Abstract: Flow-based policies such as $π_0$ are trained on a fixed set of embodiments, and transferring them to new ones is an open problem. To adapt a frozen $π_0$ to a new embodiment without additional demonstrations or parameter updates, we train a lightweight embodiment classifier to distinguish successful action chunks on the source body from those on the target body, using a few calibration tasks. On transfer tasks that $π_0$ saw on its source body but the classifier was never trained on, we nudge each denoising step of $π_0$ with the classifier's gradient, which estimates the direction from the distribution of successful actions on the source body toward that on the target body. On the LIBERO simulation benchmark with four modified controllers, embodiment-classifier guidance raises $π_0$'s average success on the target bodies from 72% to 80% across 12 suite-controller pairs, with a 31% relative reduction in failures (11 of 12 gain; 5 significant after Holm correction). In simulation, guidance is data-efficient, needing few target-body rollouts per task, which makes it promising for sim-to-real transfer, where rollouts are costly. Guidance is mixed on weak wrists and harmful on a locked wrist, when the classifier lacks data or favors infeasible motions. Collecting more target-body data with guided $π_0$ can fill such gaps: on one weak-wrist arm, guided $π_0$ solved a task on which unguided $π_0$ had zero successes in the original data, and retraining the classifier on the guided successes raised success from 52.5% to 70.7%.

</details>

Abstract: Multi-robot authoring can reduce completion time by distributing drawing work, but the resulting speedup depends on task structure and the allocation-routing pipeline. We investigate these relationships by representing drawings as geometric stroke graphs and comparing component-level (single-token) allocation with shared-token (decomposed) allocation, which permits multiple robots to work within a connected component. A simulation study of 385 drawings and teams of one to eight robots shows diminishing speedup gains under single-token allocation and larger mean speedups under the shared-token pipeline, each normalized by its own single-robot completion time. Shared-token execution also exhibits lower idle-time shares at larger team sizes, although it does not reduce completion time for every drawing. Comparisons across structural attribute groups reveal associations between structural descriptors and differences in scaling behavior, with greater separation between several attribute groups at larger team sizes. These findings characterize empirical scaling in the evaluated pipelines and highlight the importance of considering within-component workload and graph structure alongside component count. Additional materials are available at https://sites.google.com/view/multirobotauthoring/home.

Abstract: Quadruped parkour policies handle canonical obstacles well, but their training curricula often miss the irregular, compound geometry of industrial and disaster-response sites, where agile locomotion matters most. Such environments pose a hard generalization problem: even training directly on reconstructed geometry from the target site does not reliably yield policies that traverse it. Hand-designed curricula span only a few terrain families, and recent LLM-generated curricula offer little control over which policy weaknesses they target. We propose FCTS, a failure-conditioned terrain curriculum framework that uses localized traversal failures to automatically generate the terrains a policy should train on next. Each failure's location, termination mode, robot state, and surrounding terrain geometry condition this generation, and Monte Carlo Tree Search explores alternative curriculum trajectories under a fixed training budget. On terrains built from scanned real-world construction sites, FCTS raises success rate from 28.4% to 85.8% on a medium-difficulty course and from 14.9% to 44.7% on a hard course relative to the strongest parkour or LLM-curriculum baselines, and maintains higher progress on extreme terrains where full traversal is rare. Hardware experiments on a quadruped robot confirm that the learned FCTS policies transfer to the real world.

</details>

Abstract: Spatial memory enables robots to reuse past observations for localization, navigation, and question answering. Designing such memory involves jointly deciding what to retain and how to retrieve it for diverse semantic, metric, and temporal needs. We investigate whether coding agents can automate this design process through iterative implementation and evaluation. Under a fixed development budget, a Designer Agent writes and revises code for memory construction and retrieval using feedback from a fixed Query Agent. The resulting implementations are frozen before building memories from held-out observation histories, without access to downstream questions, and evaluated across five complementary task tracks using the same Query Agent. Across three independent design searches, Codex / GPT-6 Astra averages 44.23 on the equally weighted five-track score, versus 29.32 for ReMEmbR, the strongest evaluated baseline supporting all five tracks, a 50.9% relative improvement. Qualitative analysis relates performance differences to evidence retention and retrieval, while identifying grounding and evidence-use failures. These results demonstrate the potential of coding agents to automate spatial memory design, while revealing task-dependent trade-offs in performance and computational cost. Project page: https://ba-fa-3344.github.io/.

Abstract: Rigid-body motion planning is fundamental to robotic transport and manipulation, where both object position and orientation determine whether a body can move through cluttered environments. This coupling makes planning chal- lenging because translation and rotation can be constrained very differently by surrounding geometry. We propose Shape- Aware Neural Time Fields, a physics-informed neural motion planning framework that learns a reusable travel-time, or cost- to-go, representation for rigid-body motion while explicitly accounting for these distinct geometric restrictions. The learned field captures local motion difficulty in translation and rotation and is paired with a sampling-based planner that balances progress toward the goal with the cost of reaching nearby poses. We evaluate the framework across 2D and 3D rigid- body planning tasks and non-prehensile manipulation through object pushing in diverse environments. Results show substantial improvements in planning success and inference efficiency over physics-informed neural planning baselines, while remaining competitive with classical sampling-based planners at significantly lower planning times. Ablation studies further validate the contributions of both the learned representation and the proposed planning strategy. Finally, closed-loop pushing experiments under execution deviations and external disturbances demonstrate that fast replanning enables reactive and adaptive task execution.

</details>

Abstract: Precise pedicel-targeted grasping in strawberry harvesting remains a critical bottleneck due to the pedicel's small diameter (1.3-3 mm), color similarity with surrounding foliage, and precise force control required for clean abscission zone detachment. As a market-grade crop, strawberry aesthetic value makes bruise-free pedicel detachment essential over direct fruit grasping. Classical approaches struggle to unify vision models, motion planning, and contact manipulation into a robust framework, particularly in occluded canopy environments. Therefore, this work presents a visuomotor imitation learning pipeline for pedicel-targeted strawberry harvesting that unifies perception, motion planning, and contact manipulation from collecting human teleoperation demonstrations, training an Action Chunking with Transformers (ACT) policy with a ResNet-50 visual backbone, and deploying the learned policy autonomously on a Jetson AGX Orin edge platform at 15 Hz. The trained policy achieved consistent loss convergence from 2.84 to 0.091 over 100K gradient steps on 130 demonstrations. We have also established a reproducible open-source pipeline with publicly released dataset and model weights for the agricultural robotics community. Full dataset is available at, https://huggingface.co/datasets/nitindominicrai/strawberry_pedicel_grasp_teleoperation and trained policy at, https://huggingface.co/nitindominicrai/act_strawberry_pedicel. To replicate this work, all code, configurations, and deployment instructions are publicly available at, https://github.com/nitin-dominic/autonomous_strawberry_pedicel_harvesting.

### [318] [PASS-VLN: Passability-Aware Local-Command Vision-and-Language Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22812)
*Cong Zou,Xiangsheng Shi,Xuanhui Wang,Fubin Wang,Guangjun Ge,Chao Wang,Yu Wang*

Main category: cs.RO

Result: 解析错误

Abstract: Reliable vision-and-language navigation (VLN) requires not only understanding instructions but also determining whether and how a robot can physically execute them. However, existing VLN benchmarks typically assume executable instructions and focus on long-horizon tasks. This leaves body-dependent local execution, including passability and collision-free execution, largely unexamined. Evaluating these factors within long-horizon tasks would entangle local physical execution with task planning and long-term spatial memory, making navigation failures difficult to attribute. We therefore focus on grounding local navigation commands in executable motion under environmental and robot body constraints. We present PASS-VLN, a passability-aware local-command VLN benchmark and model. The benchmark holds the scene, instruction, start pose, and reference route fixed while varying robot body width, requiring target completion when the instructed passage is feasible and stopping before blockage otherwise. Our model encodes body width through obstacle inflation in a depth-derived local bird's-eye view, fuses geometry with visual and linguistic context, and predicts discrete latent action codes for continuous closed-loop execution. Experiments show that PASS-VLN improves collision-free success by approximately 12 percentage points over a strong VLN model fine-tuned on our benchmark, while adapting reach-versus-stop behavior to body constraints and generalizing to unseen body widths. When integrated with a high-level planner, PASS-VLN achieves higher success with fewer local commands, while real-world deployment further demonstrates its effectiveness as a low-level executor. The benchmark and model will be released.

</details>


### [319] [Simultaneous Force and Motion Control in Generalised Coordinates](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22813)
*Jon Woolfrey*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: 解析错误


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 解析错误

Method: 解析错误

Result: 解析错误

Conclusion: 解析错误

Abstract: Robot manipulators often come into contact with rigid objects when performing work. This imposes physical constraints on their motion. Simultaneous force & motion control was developed to regulate contact forces commensurate with these constraints, whilst projecting motion control in to the orthogonal, unconstrained directions. This is typically formulated in the task coordinates, i.e. operational space, then mapped to joint torques to command the robot. However, most constraints on the robot control, such as joint limits, collision avoidance, singularity avoidance, etc. are naturally expressed in generalised coordinates (joint space). Thus, formulating the control problem in task space makes it difficult to guarantee control feasibility. In this paper Gauss' principle of least constraint is used to solve the control as a quadratic programming (QP) problem in generalised coordinates. It is shown that the theoretical solution to this problem produces the correct constrained dynamics for accurate contact force control. Moreover, inequality constraints may be appended to ensure feasibility and safety. Simulation results were conducted in MuJoCo and compared to the operational space formulation. The proposed method is able to simultaneously maintain a desired contact force, minimise trajectory tracking error, and adhere to practical inequality constraints.

</details>

Abstract: Manipulating deformable objects toward desired 3D configurations is a fundamental capability for robots operating in unstructured physical environments. However, as the range of possible initial and target configurations grows, goal-conditioned deformable manipulation requires a robot to infer increasingly diverse manipulation behaviors in addition to precise continuous actions. We introduce DefFlow, a framework for goal-conditioned manipulation of deformable objects toward a broad range of 3D configurations. DefFlow learns from demonstrations by jointly predicting the goal-conditioned 3D point flow of the robot and deformable object. We show that this formulation better captures both the manipulation decisions, such as when and where to grasp, release, or re-grasp, and the precise continuous motions required for goal reaching, rather than predicting only the robot flow. Across rope, granular-media, and cloth manipulation settings with diverse initial and target configurations, DefFlow substantially outperforms goal-conditioned action-prediction and robot-flow-only baselines on success rates and goal-distance metrics. We further demonstrate deployment of our framework on a real robot using real point-cloud observations and unseen goal configurations.

</details>

Abstract: Generalist robot policies offer strong behavioral priors, but achieving deployment-level reliability and throughput remains difficult. Reinforcement learning (RL) can improve these policies, yet its effectiveness is constrained by the cost of online interaction. Existing methods adapt frozen flow-matching policies by treating noise space as a continuous action space. We show that this behaviorally unstructured space makes exploration inefficient, often preventing these methods from extracting meaningful learning signals on challenging tasks. We introduce AnchorQ, which enables coarse-to-fine policy improvement through a structured, refinable latent action representation. At each decision step, AnchorQ uses flow reversal to map reference behaviors to state-conditioned latent anchors, samples local particles around those anchors, and applies value-guided sampling to explore promising refinements. Our analysis shows that anchors reduce identification complexity at the cost of support bias; particle refinement trades this bias against action-class complexity; and critic accuracy determines the reliability of KL-regularized, value-guided selection. Across simulated and real-world manipulation tasks, AnchorQ outperforms state-of-the-art post-training baselines in sample efficiency, delivering a 37% improvement in real-world success rate and 97% higher simulated task throughput under the same interaction budget. Additional visualizations are available on our website https://anchorq-rl.com/.

</details>


### [322] [Structured Perception Representations for Off-road Dynamics Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22900)

Abstract: Autonomously navigating off-road terrain at high speed demands accurate control, which often relies on dynamics models that capture complex interactions between the robot and terrain. In off-road regimes, terrain interaction is a major component of the vehicle's dynamics. Prior work attempts to capture this effect by training learned models of the vehicle's dynamics, conditioned on its perception of the local environment. However, said conditioning is often overly simplistic, ignoring the spatial structure of the dynamics problem. Our approach, SpatialBEV, exploits this spatial structure. At each step, we query the bird's-eye-view (BEV) features around the predicted state, conditioning the next prediction on current geometric and semantic cues. We additionally present a benchmarking approach that mines a dataset's most difficult scenarios. On datasets collected across seasons, SpatialBEV reduces prediction error by 17.1%, compared to physics-based and learned baselines. We further evaluate on hardware on high-speed ($\geq$10 m/s) and geometrically challenging courses with a 25.3% reduction in maximum cross-track error. More details can be found at https://theairlab.org/spatial-bev-dynamics .

</details>


### [323] [SenseSwarm: Swarm Exploration via Neural Structure Estimation with Shared World-Anticipation for Rapid Mapping](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22905)
*Haohua Que,Haojia Gao,Mingkai Liu,Hoiian Au,Qian Zhang,Jiayue Xie,Yule Cai,Bokui Chen,Xueqian Wang,Fei Qiao*

Abstract: A robot swarm covers a building faster with more robots, but the map is only as complete as the space the robots have physically visited. We target the amount of an unknown building a decentralized swarm knows at each instant, ahead of physical coverage. SenseSwarm serves a lightweight learned map predictor on the swarm's shared occupancy map and folds the predicted structure back into a stigmergic guidance field. Its 709K-parameter predictor is fine-tuned on the swarm's own multi-front observations, which we show is necessary: a single-robot predictor collapses on such views. We build a benchmark of twelve real building floors from HouseExpo, HKUST, and KTH, up to 24,000 square meters, and run 1,620 simulations across nine coordination methods, swarm sizes 32 to 512, and three seeds. On physical coverage the swarm is near parity with a greedy frontier planner, as expected. On map reconstruction, every non-predictive baseline needs 1.3 to 1.9 times longer to reach an 80 percent known map, and SenseSwarm holds an equal-time area lead of about 670 square meters, up to 1,359. Folding the prediction into the shared field matters: the same predictor applied per robot matches our speed but suffers a 6.9 times higher inter-robot contact rate at 512 robots. The prediction is computed once per shared map, so its cost stayed flat from 32 to 512 robots.

</details>


### [324] [Ego2Contact: Predicting Touch from Egocentric Human Video for Contact-Rich Robot Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22932)

Abstract: Contact locations and interaction forces are critical for contact-rich robot manipulation but are often ambiguous from vision alone. Measuring them on robots requires specialized tactile hardware and platform-specific data collection, which keeps robot tactile data scarce. Human egocentric video is far easier to scale but lacks touch. Recent predictors trained on egocentric recordings paired with tactile-glove measurements can predict this missing signal from video alone. These predictors, however, remain confined to human hands, which differ from robot hands in appearance and tactile layout. We present Ego2Contact, which learns a visual-tactile prior from human interactions and transfers it to robot hands. Its predictor fuses temporal scene context with hand-local visual features to infer bimanual tactile fields from the RGB video. Anatomical region correspondences align varying robot tactile layouts with the predictor's canonical grid, turning robot measurements into fine-tuning targets and predictions into policy inputs. Ego2Contact outperforms prior tactile predictors on human data, including unseen objects, and on robot data after adaptation. The adapted predictor provides tactile inputs for both simulated reinforcement learning and real-robot policy post-training. In simulation, policies using predicted touch outperform non-tactile baselines and recover part of the benefit of ground-truth tactile sensing. These results show that human-learned tactile priors can supply touch when direct tactile sensing is limited.

</details>

Abstract: Vision-language-action (VLA) models have shown strong promise for generalist robotic manipulation, but their inference cost remains a major bottleneck for real-time control. This limitation is especially pronounced in flow-matching VLAs such as $π_{0.5}$, where the action expert is evaluated repeatedly during iterative denoising. We introduce MambaAct, an efficient Mamba action expert for flow-matching VLA policies. MambaAct retains the pretrained visual-language prefix encoder of $π_{0.5}$, while replacing its transformer-based action expert with a compact Mamba-based action expert. Within this expert, Mamba serves as the action mixer that models dependencies across the noisy continuous-action sequence. To preserve visual-language conditioning without repeated token-level cross-attention, MambaAct uses a lightweight Context Compressor to map Vision-language model (VLM) prefix tokens into a compact conditioning signal that gates and modulates each Mamba action block. MambaAct uses a $10\times$ smaller action expert and reduces flow-matching denoising latency by $64.8\%$ on LIBERO and $58.8\%$ in real-world deployment, while improving mean LIBERO success by $1.08$ percentage points across three independent fine-tuning seeds and maintaining gains on LIBERO-Plus and real-world benchmarks. Our code and model weights are publicly available at https://github.com/giakhuyendihoc/MambaAct.

</details>


### [326] [GR-VIP: Geometric representation conditioning for precise visuomotor policy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22997)

Abstract: Achieving precise terminal position and heading alignment remains a significant challenge for learning-based mobile robot control. Recent vision-based learning methods have shown promise for precise target-relative control and generalization to unseen environments with pretrained visual encoders, but general-purpose visual representations are not specifically designed to capture the fine-grained spatial differences between current and goal views required for precise control, and real-world demonstrations combining millimeter-scale terminal accuracy with zero-shot transfer across environments remain limited. To address these challenges, we propose Geometric Representation Conditioning for Precise Visuomotor Policy (GR-VIP), a goal-conditioned visuomotor policy that leverages a visual encoder pretrained for relative camera pose estimation. Rather than using the final relative-pose estimate, GR-VIP conditions action prediction on the dense relational features produced before pose regression, preserving fine-grained cross-view information for learning corrective actions from demonstrations. We evaluate GR-VIP on a real-world docking task and demonstrate high docking success rates and millimeter-scale terminal accuracy in both the training and unseen environments, without additional training for transfer. Furthermore, supplementary experiments illustrate the policy's ability to align with non-docking targets and complete docking under a tested low-light condition.

</details>

Abstract: Generative policies can synthesize robot behaviors, yet controlling and editing them through diverse spatiotemporal references remains challenging. In the real world, human intent requires robot behaviors that satisfy constraints on where, when, and how actions are executed. Our key insight is that spatiotemporal control references, such as keypoints, flows, orientations, and keyposes, can be viewed as partial specifications of the same temporal Cartesian action space. We introduce the MAP (Masked Action Space) interface to unify these action references and formulate controllable manipulation as an action inpainting problem. Our Inpainting Policy (IP) reconstructs missing action dimensions of the end-effector, combining progress-guided alignment with multi-mask training to support unseen action-constraint combinations. We further adopt a training-free RePaint variant that iteratively inpaints the action during denoising to reduce control error. On ManiSkill and LIBERO-10, our policy achieves average task success rates of 92.67% and 64.67%, respectively.Real-world experiments demonstrate spatiotemporal control with average positional and rotational errors of 44.3 mm and 7°, respectively, showing that our policy can use diverse references to guide executable robot behaviors.

</details>

Abstract: The physical world has continuous state and action spaces, yet robot planning often benefits from hybrid discrete-continuous formulations that explicitly expose combinatorial structure, such as contact modes or discrete primitive actions. Existing methods typically rely on human-defined symbol sets or symbols derived from language supervision, without asking what makes a symbol computationally useful. In this paper, we take an inference-algorithm--centric view to define usefulness: a good symbol should expose the discrete commitments that simplify solving the original planning problem. We first introduce the concept of solver-sufficient symbolization by studying finite-horizon piecewise-affine systems, for which solver sufficiency can be precisely characterized, and develop a counterexample-guided algorithm for symbol invention. We then extend this principle to invent symbols for optimization objectives parameterized by general neural networks as energy functions, showing that solver-sufficient symbols can nevertheless be induced from their continuous optimization landscapes. Experiments on simulated and real-robot tasks show that the resulting symbols reduce solving cost while preserving solution quality, while also improving interpretability and steerability. Furthermore, our theoretical and algorithmic insights help explain why certain human-defined symbols are computationally useful.

</details>

Abstract: Low-cost passive leader arms enable scalable data collection for imitation learning, but their lack of restoring forces leaves redundant joints unconstrained, yielding highly inconsistent joint-space trajectories. We address this with a low-cost passive joint module (an adjustable mechanical clutch and a 3D-printed spiral spring) that pulls each joint toward a user-defined neutral posture. This passive restoring force acts as a physical prior equivalent to joint-space $L_2$ regularization (``Hardware-as-Regularization''), improving kinematic consistency with shorter completion time than an unregularized arm and preserved movement smoothness. Experiments show substantial variance reduction over a naive passive arm, trajectory consistency on par with an active virtual spring of the same actuator class, and substantially higher downstream success rates (e.g., 73.3% vs. 36.7% on Block Stacking); a stiffness sweep shows consistency degrading monotonically as stiffness decreases, confirming that mechanical stiffness behaves like a regularization weight.

</details>

Abstract: Vision-language-action (VLA) models are rapidly expanding what individual robots can accomplish, yet many real-world manipulation settings require multiple arms to operate as a coordinated system. Existing approaches organize multi-arm coordination in different ways, including centralized joint policies, high-level task or role assignment, and locally executed policies that react to teammates. However, how complete action-producing VLAs should explicitly interact with one another during action generation remains largely unexplored. We introduce VLA-Fabric, which organizes one complete VLA per arm while preserving local perception and action ownership, and enables coordination through learned interaction across policy boundaries. We first systematically study where such interaction should occur, how peer information should be aggregated, and how the interaction should be learned, revealing complementary coordination functions spanning shared context, peer-specific latent information, and action-stage interaction. We then re-instantiate these functions in a structurally different VLA architecture, showing that the coordination principle is not tied to a particular tensor layout or backbone implementation. Finally, we compose teams of three and four arms and evaluate them in simulation and physical experiments. On all four physical tasks, Full interaction improves success over independently trained local policies by 16--40 percentage points. Together, these results suggest that explicit interaction among complete VLA policies offers a scalable path toward networked embodied intelligence.

</details>


### [331] [PASS: A Trustworthy Precision Approach Simulation System for CAT III Integrity Algorithm Development](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23019)

Abstract: For fixed-wing aircraft, reliable localization during approach and landing is critical to flight safety. Visual--inertial navigation is promising, but aircraft navigation also requires integrity: trustworthy outputs and timely warnings when they should not be used. Real approach data are costly to collect, while many visual errors and failures required for integrity algorithm development are difficult to reproduce safely. We present PASS, a trustworthy Precision Approach Simulation System for CAT III integrity algorithm development. In a unified temporal and geometric framework, PASS integrates configurable airport environments, prescribed trajectories, inertial simulation, calibrated stereo images, and continuous runway-relative ground truth. It supports navigation and integrity research through controllable sensor errors and environmental variations, computable visual projection errors, deterministic runway geometry priors, and condition-labeled data for statistical error modeling. We further propose Sim-to-Real Scene Trustworthiness (SRST), a measure based on DINOv2 feature distributions and UMAP, to quantify sim-to-real closeness. Experiments with representative stereo--inertial estimators demonstrate the usability of the generated sequences and quantify localization errors across algorithms and environments. PASS provides a controllable foundation for developing and evaluating visual--inertial navigation and integrity algorithms for fixed-wing precision approaches up to CAT III requirements.

</details>

Abstract: Vision-language-action (VLA) models and world-action models (WAMs) have emerged as promising approaches to robot learning and control. These models integrate multimodal perception and action generation, but differ in their architectures, output representations, and learning objectives. In this paper, we present a unified, architecture-agnostic probabilistic formulation for embodied foundation models based on stochastic kernels. Our formulation has two key components: (i) we characterize embodied foundation models through conditional output distributions and action-realization kernels, which together induce task-conditioned robot policies; and (ii) we connect pretraining and post-training to policy deployment, accommodating model-specific pretraining objectives and offline or online post-training strategies. We instantiate the formulation for OpenVLA and DreamZero, covering autoregressive action-token prediction and joint video-action generation.

</details>


### [333] [Foundation Models for Autonomous Space Robotics: Architectures, Physical Adaptation, and Validation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23068)

Abstract: Space robots must interpret tasks and act despite unfamiliar dynamics, difficult sensing conditions, and limited opportunities for human intervention. Foundation models offer reusable perceptual and behavioral knowledge, but their value depends on how that knowledge reaches physical execution. This critical review follows space-specific requirements through perception, prediction, planning, and action to deployment and validation, emphasizing orbital manipulation and proximity operations. Terrestrial comparisons establish benefits of particular pretraining stages and model interfaces; their transfer to space dynamics and constrained onboard operation remains insufficiently tested. Event cameras offer timely motion observations across strong illumination contrasts, while spiking neural networks provide possible routes to sparse local computation. We examine how sensor degradation, state estimation and computing choices affect feedback and recovery. Higher action throughput need not yield fresher feedback; lower processor power can also coexist with higher energy per inference. We organize validation as an iterative sim-to-ground-to-space pathway, connecting physical tests with telemetry-based diagnosis and closed-loop revalidation. A satellite-capture example links observations to approach, contact and recovery, while a corresponding protocol separates generalization from adaptation with limited target-domain interaction. These comparisons identify what models already contribute and the physical evidence needed to support autonomous space operation.

</details>

Abstract: Heterogeneous robot teams enable complex, long-horizon tasks by combining complementary sensing, mobility, and manipulation capabilities across multiple embodiments. Yet existing systems struggle to retain execution-time corrections as reusable experience, maintain task-relevant context memory across space and time for reliable handoffs, and explicitly represent the inter-robot dependencies required for structured collaboration. In order to address these challenges, we present InstAgent, a recursively self-improving (RSI) Agent Operating System for heterogeneous robot collaboration. InstAgent organizes team behaviors as agentic behavior trees (BTs) that explicitly model capability assignment, inter-robot dependencies and handoff checks. The RSI mechanism updates these collaboration programs from execution feedback and preserves successful revisions for reuse in future tasks, while shared multimodal spatio-temporal memory provides context for planning and handoff validation. Experiments on 20 real-world multi-robot tasks with three high-level models show consistent gains in task success, first-attempt completion, and execution efficiency.

### [335] [Bio-Inspired Magnetic Navigation Using Gradient Descent With Rao-Blackwellized Particle Filtering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23097)

Abstract: Navigation in GPS-denied environments is commonly based on magnetic maps, which are used to correct trajectory drift and enable relocalization. However, such navigation systems suffer from a strong dependence on magnetic maps that are complex, heavy, and costly to construct. Bio-inspired magnetic navigation (BIMN) does not rely on magnetic maps, making it simpler and more frugal. In this paper, we present a gradient descent BIMN method based on the Rao-Blackwellized particle filter (RBPF) for local gradient estimation. We show that magnetic navigation can be performed without magnetic maps, as gradient descent naturally attracts the system toward the destination, resulting in a simple and memory-efficient approach.

</details>


### [336] [GRASP: Global-Local Representation Alignment and Spatial-Physical Grounding for Bimanual Robotic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23123)
*Zeyuan Ren,Guanxun Zhao,Qi Li,Hao Wang,Wenlong Liao,Keyi Luo,Xijing Zhang,Zhiyuan Zhang,Jingye Cui,Qibin Gu,Zhichen Wang,Yucheng Hu,Liqiao Xia,Nan Bu,Zhengde Ma,Rankun Li,Xue Yang,Wende Xie*

Abstract: Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models that directly regress joint-space actions avoid the deployment-time cost of inverse kinematics. However, their training objectives remain misaligned with task success, as joint-space losses ignore the unequal physical consequences of joint errors and visual conditioning lacks explicit focus on hand-object interaction regions. To address these issues, we propose a training-phase prior injection framework for fine-grained bimanual manipulation. First, a physics-grounded flow-matching loss maps the implicit clean-action estimate at every noise level through differentiable forward kinematics (FK) and supervises the resulting Tool Center Point (TCP) pose and inter-arm relative pose, with a bounded noise-level weighting that compensates gradient decay. Second, we introduce a global prediction query group distilled from a frozen DINOv3 teacher through future-frame feature prediction, together with a local interaction query supervised by Grounding DINO at the attention level. Both teachers are removed at inference, and the deployment pipeline is that of a standard joint-space flow policy, adding only the learned query pathway. Simulation and real-robot experiments on long-horizon bimanual tasks show that the two priors are complementary across different deployment settings. The full model excels in in-distribution deployment, while the distilled query branch is preferable under strong visual randomization.

</details>


### [337] [BeJEPA: A Goal-Conditioned Humanoid Behavior World Model via Unsupervised RL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23127)
*Liyun Yan,Haoyang Li,Yang Zhang,Jialing Zhu,Zhanxiang Cao,Zihao Zhou,Keqi Zhu,Yizhi Chen,Huang Chuxuan,Shengcheng Fu,Zhong Jiangwei,Qiao Sun,Chen Zhang,Yue Gao*

Abstract: Humanoid control requires coordinating whole-body posture with position and heading in the world frame. Mainstream controllers imitate trajectories in the root frame, but struggle to regain the target world pose after displacement. We introduce BeJEPA, a goal-conditioned Behavior World Model learned through unsupervised reinforcement learning from human motion priors and robot interaction. BeJEPA learns a shared representation of body configuration and world placement through a joint-embedding predictive architecture (JEPA). A Dynamic Predictor predicts the next state embedding under an action, while a Successor Predictor summarizes a goal-conditioned policy's expected discounted future state embeddings in a single vector. During joint training, Dynamic grounds the initial representation in observed physical transitions, while Successor increasingly organizes it around long-term behavior. Their composition guides policy improvement through one physical prediction and one behavioral summary, without long imagined rollouts. In 20-second motion-tracking experiments, BeJEPA with online action refinement using Dynamic outperforms all evaluated baselines in global body-position error, reducing it on AMASS by 61.7% relative to BFM-Zero with world-pose inputs and 58.7% relative to SONIC. Physical deployment on a Unitree G1 demonstrates joint attainment of posture, world position, and heading goals, recovery from the ground, and closed-loop return after disturbances and goal changes. Project page: https://ylyem9x.github.io/BeJEPA-project.

</details>


### [338] [SCOPE: Selective Certification of Program Edits for Embodied Heuristic Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23128)

Abstract: Embodied heuristic learning must balance strategic adaptation with the preservation of previously successful behavior. Local repairs may leave structural failures unresolved, whereas broader revisions can introduce regressions that are costly to detect through repeated environment interaction. We introduce SCOPE (Selective Certification Of Program Edits), which uses failed and successful executions as complementary evidence for program revision. Frontier-guided program search annotates a shared diagnostic graph with execution evidence to distinguish supported progress from unresolved checks. Selective behavioral certification uses stateful API replay to restore recorded responses and mutations, checking whether edits preserve selected historical successes. Diagnosis and certification feedback, supplemented by sparse environment diagnostics, guide consecutive repairs across configurations. SCOPE outperforms the strongest baseline in both simulation and real-world tasks. On LIBERO-PRO and Robosuite, it achieves mean success rates of 80.66% and 84.48%, with respective gains of 6.51 and 3.48 percentage points. Our code is available at https://github.com/VDIGPKU/SCOPE.

</details>


### [339] [ORD-WM: Learning Object-Centric Rigid Dynamics for 3D World Modeling in Robotic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23156)
*Yueyang Weng,Xiaopeng Zhang,Yuxi Ou,Yongjin Mu,Chengzhi Zhao,Yanjie Li*

Abstract: World models provide robots with a predictive mechanism for reasoning about how actions change object states in manipulation tasks. However, existing image-based and dense 3D world models do not explicitly impose object-level rigid motion, leaving rigid-object geometry preservation to be learned implicitly and making multi-step rollouts susceptible to shape drift or object-level motion inconsistency. To address this, we propose ORD-WM, a 3D world model that learns object-centric rigid dynamics for robotic manipulation. ORD-WM learns a vector-neuron variational autoencoder supervised by signed distance fields, which encodes each object point cloud as a geometry-aware latent representation composed of 3D vector features, and uses an action-conditioned dynamics model to predict object-wise SE(3) motion increments. Rather than generating unconstrained next latent states, ORD-WM analytically applies predicted object-wise rigid transformations to the current latent states, separating persistent object geometry from pose changes. Experiments show that ORD-WM enables stable, geometry-preserving multi-step prediction and supports efficient real-time replanning for closed-loop model predictive control in simulated and real-world manipulation scenarios.

</details>


### [340] [SimpleVLN: Towards Simple and Frontier Vision-Language Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23203)
*Lingyi Hong,Weiren Cai,Jiayu Chen,Xinyu Zhou,Bailin He,Yunfei Guo,Qiao Sun,Boyu Ma,Jianfei Yang,Wenqiang Zhang*

Abstract: Vision-and-Language Navigation (VLN) requires embodied agents to ground language in visual observations and make long-horizon decisions. Existing methods often address these demands through task-specific architectures. As multimodal large language models (MLLMs) become increasingly capable, an important question remains: Have we truly unlocked the potential of native MLLMs for VLN before introducing task-specific architecture? We present SimpleVLN, an architecture-preserving framework developed through studies of navigation formulation, data construction, and optimization. At the formulation level, SimpleVLN combines single-turn decision inputs with fixed-budget global-local memory to efficiently use navigation history without unbounded context growth, while action chunks enable efficient output of multiple primitive actions per model invocation. For data construction, we combine expert demonstrations with policy-dependent DAgger experience and develop a scalable synthesis pipeline that generates large-scale tasks for reinforcement learning. At the optimization level, a progressive IL-DAgger-RL pipeline advances from action imitation to task-level outcome optimization. Comprehensive experiments validate the effectiveness of these three aspects. We instantiate SimpleVLN across multiple MLLM backbones and model sizes, demonstrating its generality. SimpleVLN achieves 76.7% and 80.1% success rates on the R2R-CE and RxR-CE splits, respectively, substantially outperforming existing methods. These results show that the navigation capabilities of native MLLMs can be effectively unlocked without task-specific architectural designs.

</details>


### [341] [Expand-and-Prune: Test-Time Candidate-Space Adaptation for End-to-End Autonomous Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23262)
*Fabian Schmidt,Karol Fedurko,Markus Enzweiler,Abhinav Valada*

Abstract: Candidate-based end-to-end driving planners reason over a finite set of trajectory hypotheses. However, for sparse planners, a limited candidate set may provide insufficient support for scene-relevant maneuvers, while fixed downstream candidate budgets do not account for prediction uncertainty. To address these challenges, we introduce Expand-and-Prune, a test-time framework that jointly adapts candidate support and capacity without updating learned parameters. The framework expands the native candidate set using semantic driving intent and temporally propagated predictions and then uses uncertainty in intermediate trajectory scores to adaptively prune the resulting candidates. Across four heterogeneous candidate-based planners and four benchmarks, Expand-and-Prune consistently improves planning performance. It achieves state-of-the-art results on LangAuto, increasing the Driving Score from 68.2 to 75.9, while providing further improvement on Bench2Drive, Fail2Drive, and NAVSIMv2, with particularly strong improvements in out-of-distribution scenarios. Moreover, in its full instantiation, 72.5% of selected trajectories originate from the newly introduced intent-guided or temporal candidates, confirming that candidate-space expansion provides useful hypotheses beyond those generated natively by the planner. The code is available at https://github.com/iis-esslingen/Expand-and-Prune.

</details>

Abstract: Vision--Language--Action (VLA) models promise generalist robotic agents, yet consolidating skills learned across heterogeneous tasks and environments remains difficult: training data is fragmented across platforms, joint multi-task training suffers from interference, and model merging tends to introduce parameter conflicts or require architectural changes. We instead advocate a paradigm in which skills are first learned independently and then consolidated post hoc. To this end, we propose NestedVLA, which replaces parameter aggregation with parameter generation: a nested hypernetwork, conditioned on the target task, synthesizes task-adaptive action-expert parameters that compose with a frozen base VLA. Task-specific compressed VLM residuals are retained separately. This formulation enables flexible task-conditioned skill reuse while mitigating interference. Across three RL benchmarks (MetaWorld, ManiSkill, CALVIN), NestedVLA outperforms the strongest model-merging baseline by 41 pp on average, matches per-task fine-tuning within 1 pp on ManiSkill, and substantially closes the gap on CALVIN, pointing toward a storage-efficient route to skill consolidation. Code is available at https://github.com/WanyuGroup/NeurIPS2026-NestedVLA.

</details>

Abstract: We present LY-GWM-RoboCasa-Human300, a policy that combines a Human300-fine-tuned GR00T-N1.5 action generator, an action-conditioned graph dynamics model, and a learned binary state-reward scorer. At inference, the policy samples four action chunks, predicts the future latent features and robot state for each, and executes the candidate with the highest predicted-state reward logit. Predictions therefore influence the actions sent to the environment. The evaluated system uses a 16-step prediction horizon and keeps all three components frozen during evaluation. In the local evaluation on 50 RoboCasa 1.0.1 tasks in the pretrain kitchen split, with 50 episodes per task, the system records 207/2,500 successes (8.28%), compared with 181/2,500 (7.24%) in the historical self-trained GR00T-N1.5 baseline. The observed difference is 26 successes, or 1.04 percentage points. The atomic group improves from 141/900 to 150/900. Separately, the RoboCasa maintainers re-evaluated the submission and reported 354/1,500 successes (23.6%) across 50 tasks, with leaderboard rates of 48.3%, 16.3%, and 3.1% for Atomic-Seen, Composite-Seen, and Composite-Unseen. The local and official results are reported separately; their difference is not attributed to a model improvement. Recorded local audits identify 212,700 changed environment-input chunks, including 28,223 in atomic tasks. We describe the actual training stages, implemented inference mechanism, evaluation provenance, and public reproduction entrypoints. These results document an active prediction-and-selection system and a positive historical difference.

</details>


### [344] [SkyAgent: Revisable Target Memory for Continuous Aerial Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23293)

Abstract: Continuous aerial search builds target hypotheses from sparse, viewpoint-dependent observations. When later evidence contradicts a stored hypothesis, however, the candidate can remain available to the planner and continue triggering approach or verification actions. We call this trajectory-level failure false candidate persistence (FCP). We equip SkyAgent with Revisable Target Memory (RTM), which associates evidence with persistent candidates, discounts correlated views, and revises each candidate through Tentative, Confirmed, and Rejected states. The key interface binds every generated waypoint to its source candidate: rejection therefore invalidates stale goals, while conservative reactivation protects temporarily occluded targets. Across four splits from the CityAVOS, UAV-ON, and OpenUAV benchmarks, our SkyAgent equipped with RTM outperforms all competitors, improving SR by 2.90--6.31 points while substantially reducing Behavior-FCP and post-contradiction travel with a low false-rejection rate. Across 35 matched real-world DJI scenarios per method, RTM consistently improves task success and behavioral reliability, further validating its state-to-action correction under physical deployment conditions.

</details>

Abstract: Recent advances in Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models and World Action Models (WAMs) have expanded the manipulation and whole body capabilities of individual robots. Yet a single robot remains limited by its embodiment, and many real world tasks require capabilities that can only emerge through cooperation. Existing multi robot frameworks largely emphasize task decomposition, allocation, and replanning, while providing limited support for adapting executable coordination when physical execution deviates from the intended behavior. We present CoEvolve, a framework that represents team coordination as executable code over fixed robot motor skills. CoEvolve introduces Verifier Guided Coordination Evolution, where a multimodal foundation model instantiates coordination strategies through motion primitives, semantic objectives, temporary robot roles, and bounded execution parameters. Physical verification evaluates each executed transition, rejected behaviors trigger structured coordination revision from an accepted checkpoint, and verified experience is retained to guide subsequent decisions. We evaluate CoEvolve with two simulated Unitree G1 humanoids transporting a freely supported rigid payload through constrained corridors. Across 15 base task runs with three multimodal backbone families, CoEvolve completes 8 tasks, with every backbone achieving end to end success. Additional evaluations yield verified placement on two additional corridor layouts and after both same-backbone and cross-backbone experience initialization, while exposing persistent limitations in motion precision and contact stability. These results support executable coordination as a practical interface for adapting long horizon multi robot collaboration under shared physical constraints.

</details>

Abstract: Long-horizon robotic tasks expose a fundamental limitation of current Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models because their restricted temporal context pre-vents effective accumulation and reuse of past experiences. As a result, memory has become a fundamental component for enabling embodied agents to perform long-horizon reasoning and adaptive decision-making. This survey presents a systematic taxonomy of VLA memory mechanisms based on memory scope and persistence, categorizing existing approaches into five representative paradigms and one emerging frontier. Based on this taxonomy, we compare representative VLA memory systems in terms of memory characteristics, architectural com-plexity, and open-source maturity, revealing a clear trade-off in which increasing architectural complexity is generally accompanied by decreasing open-source ma-turity. Finally, we discuss the key challenges in building full-lifecycle memory systems, including efficient memory management and lifelong knowledge evolu-tion. Overall, this survey provides a unified perspective on memory architectures for VLA agents and offers insights into the development of lifelong embodied in-telligence.

</details>


### [347] [Beyond Success Rate: Interventional Evaluation of Memory in Vision-Language-Action Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23372)
*Keyuan Luo,Yi Xie,Wanyun Ling,Yuanzhe Li,Ziyue Li,Zhuoqi Zeng*

Main category: cs.RO

Result: 解析错误

Conclusion: 解析错误

Abstract: When we say that a robot uses memory, does that memory actually play a role? Success-rate ablations alone cannot establish the use of task-relevant facts: they change information and computation together, while aggregate rates can hide offsetting gains and losses. We propose an Interventional Memory Evaluation Protocol (IMEP) for vision-language-action (VLA) systems to address four questions: (1) Does memory provide a net benefit? (2) Under what conditions does memory help? (3) Do changes to memory alter task outcomes? (4) Are these effects specific to task-relevant facts? At the retrieval interface, we compare target-fact substitutions with fact-preserving replacements, minimal numerical perturbations, and exact replicas, while assessing decision correctness separately from motor execution success. We evaluate within-episode, cross-episode, and agentic memory. In cross-episode learned-bank experiments, a one-unit-in-the-last-place (ULP) intervention, including its installation procedure, changes 11.9% of seed-paired outcomes, while changing key facts produces no clear additional effect over fact-preserving replacements. To examine when memory helps, we introduce ChainBench, where correct decisions require historical information unavailable in current observations. The full memory configuration raises task success from 39.1% to 74.3%, matching the oracle-decision condition under shared recorded executions. These results show that correct history can support robot decisions, but establishing whether a memory system realizes this benefit requires fact-specific evidence beyond aggregate success rates.

</details>

Abstract: World-action models jointly predict actions and future world states, but long-horizon manipulation further requires language reasoning to plan subtask objectives, assess execution progress, and guide action selection. We present LAW, a closed-loop VLM--WAM framework that unifies language reasoning, action generation, and world modeling through semantic subtask progress. Within the LAW agent, a hybrid-expert vision-language model plans the remaining subtasks, estimates the values of and fuses actions proposed by a world-action model, and asynchronously assesses execution status to support continued execution and subtask transitions. Within the world-action model, Mixture-of-Transformer Experts integrates modality-specific and shared computation for joint video--action modeling. Dual-stream hierarchical visual memory maintains temporal context for both models, while subtask-local value learning aligns action evaluation with semantic objectives and preserves supervision from successful prefixes of failed trajectories. LAW achieves 94.21% average success across 50 RoboTwin tasks. Replacing the WAM backbone with OpenWAM-$alpha$ further raises the average success rate to 95.56%, establishing a new open-source state of the art on RoboTwin. Despite having only 2B parameters, our VLM also outperforms variants using Embodied-R1.5 and GPT-5.5 High. On three long-horizon real-robot tasks, LAW achieves 65% average success, compared with 28.33% for $pi_{0.5}$ and 25% for Fast-WAM.

</details>


### [349] [Look Before You Act: Bridging Active Perception and Vision-Language-Action Models via Observation-Aligned Data Adaptation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23378)
*Jiawei Geng,Zhenyang Liu,Yuzheng Wu,Yongchong Gu,Yanwei Fu*

Abstract: Robots manipulating objects in clutter must decide not only how to act, but also where to look. Although active camera motion can expose an occluded target, it may move the robot into an initial observation-state distribution that differs from the manipulation policy's training distribution. We identify this problem as first-frame distribution sensitivity: under perturbed starts in LIBERO, naively prepending camera repositioning to pi_0.5 reduces success from 46.3% to 11.5%. We introduce ActiveAlign, a data-adaptation framework that trains a Vision-Language-Action (VLA) policy from the states at which active perception terminates. A learned viewpoint module first generates a camera trajectory. Offline, ActiveAlign uses closed-loop alignment to connect the trajectory endpoint to an existing demonstration, replays the remaining manipulation trajectory, and retains successful continuations for fine-tuning. At deployment, the same viewpoint module precedes the adapted VLA without modifying its architecture. Across 40 LIBERO tasks, ActiveAlign achieves 70.7% success with pi_0 and 72.7% with pi_0.5, outperforming their standard-training baselines by 40.0% and 26.4% respectively. Matched-state and data-matched controls support gains beyond prefix shortening and generic viewpoint augmentation; OpenVLA and GR00T N1 also show the same trend. Success rate under physical occlusion in real world increases from 35/90 to 83/90. These results show that active perception benefits manipulation when policy training is aligned with the observation and robot-state distribution induced by viewpoint selection.

</details>


### [350] [No Eyes, No Problem: What Does Touch Contribute to Blind Baoding Ball Manipulation?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23438)
*Ayush Deshmukh,Nalin Agarwal,Elle Miller,Sethu Vijayakumar*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: 解析错误

Result: 解析错误

Abstract: Humans manipulate objects within their hands through touch alone with remarkable ease, but for robots, achieving sustained real-world manipulation without vision remains an open challenge. Although reinforcement learning (RL) can generate sophisticated blind manipulation policies in simulation, transferring them to a real dexterous hand remains hindered by continuously changing contacts, motion-induced tactile information drift, and the gap between simulated and physical dynamics. We study this problem through Baoding ball rotation on the Shadow Dexterous Hand Lite, using only proprioception and binary tactile feedback as observations. We achieve successful zero-shot transfer through a combination of a sensor-aligned, coupling-aware embodiment in simulation, tactile calibration, and two novel episodic domain randomisations: slew-rate action limits and STuck-AT Taxels. On the physical hand, the policy achieves sustained blind Baoding rotation, completing 225 consecutive $\mathbf{180^\circ}$ rotations (112.5 full rotations) at $\sim0.36 \;\text{rot/s}$ in a single continuous trial without any vision or object-state information. Furthermore, we evaluate the contribution of tactile sensing relative to proprioception alone and identify the hand regions where tactile information is most important. This is the strongest reported hardware result for Baoding ball manipulation in both rotation count and speed, irrespective of the sensing modality used. By effectively bridging the sim-to-real gap, our framework sets a new benchmark for sustained tactile-driven dexterous manipulation on physical hardware.

</details>


### [351] [AWAM: Active Trajectory-Conditioned View Imagination for Robust Robot Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23396)

Abstract: Rapid progress in embodied intelligence has enabled increasingly capable robots to follow language instructions and manipulate diverse objects. However, strong results in structured environments do not automatically transfer to open-world conditions, where clutter, occlusion, and changing viewpoints can obscure the spatial relationships required for action. To address this perceptual gap, a virtual active-perception paradigm is introduced. AWAM, an Active World-Action Model actively plans where to observe and imagines what will be seen along the planned trajectory before acting. Task-related virtual camera motion is predicted and used as a structured query into a geometry-conditioned world model, producing a reusable spatial prior for manipulation, which is later compressed into a fixed memory and fused with live observations through cross-attention for diffusion-based action prediction. AWAM achieves competitive success rates with 75.9% on LIBERO-Occ, 62.4% on LIBERO-Plus, 76.5% on RLBench-OG Occlusion1, and 94.4% in real-robot trials. These results support improved robustness to the evaluated occlusions and distribution shifts, addressing part of the gap between structured manipulation and less controlled environments.

</details>

Abstract: Agents built on vision language models (VLMs) now compete with learned policies on robot manipulation leaderboards. We ask how far a coding agent can go when it controls a robot instead of a computer, and where it stops. Our system, HexaShell, keeps the harness thin: it only turns the agent's commands into robot motion. The agent drives a robot with two arms through one command line. It sees three RGB cameras and knows their calibration. After each failed attempt, the agent reads the log, finds the cause, and writes or fixes small programs called tools. In this way it keeps what it learns as code, as a coding agent does when it fixes a failing test. The tools may use only what the robot observes. They use triangulation to find where objects are, relative to each other and to the gripper. If two cameras see the same point, the point lies where the two camera rays meet. If a point lies on a known plane, such as the table or the top of a block, it lies where its ray meets that plane. When the head camera is not enough, the robot moves a wrist camera to get a second view. We build the tools on only ten layouts of one seed per task, then freeze them and test them on ten new layouts of the same seed. We use Codex with GPT-6-Astra at medium reasoning effort. On all 42 RoboDojo tasks, HexaShell reaches a success rate of 39.7% and a progress score of 48.0, averaged in the same way as the leaderboard. This is a new state of the art on RoboDojo. The limit is contact, not reasoning: pushing reaches 92.5% and memory tasks 65.0%, but tight insertion only 8.3%. When the agent fails, it usually sees the failure but cannot make the motion work. We open source the tools evolved so far at https://github.com/HexaFuture/HexaShell.

</details>


### [353] [FLoTS-SLAM: Optical Flow-Derived Uncertainty for Monocular Dynamic SLAM with Two-Stage Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23496)
*Haorui Du,Lipu Zhou*

Method: 解析错误

Result: 解析错误

Conclusion: 解析错误

Abstract: Monocular simultaneous localization and mapping (SLAM) remains challenging in dynamic environments. Real-time dynamic object identification in such environments is a critical problem. Recent approaches estimate per-pixel uncertainty from rendering residuals or multiview feature inconsistency to identify dynamic regions. These methods lack direct motion evidence, and their accuracy may degrade when reconstruction is inaccurate or features are ambiguous. In addition, such uncertainty merely downweights pixels in bundle adjustment (BA), so inaccurate uncertainty estimates can still impair camera pose estimation and 3D reconstruction. Accordingly, we present a monocular RGB SLAM system for dynamic scenes. Specifically, a motion uncertainty estimation method is proposed to derive uncertainty from the residual between optical flow and camera motion induced flow, directly reflecting object motion. The estimation varies with frame alignment accuracy, reducing false detections and yielding accurate pixel-level masks of dynamic regions. To make BA more robust, we estimate camera poses from high confidence pixels. These poses are then used to reproject dynamic pixels during joint refinement of poses and depth, reducing the influence of object motion on both pose and depth updates. Moreover, we provide a quantitative evaluation of dynamic masks and analyze how these masks affect the downstream tracking and reconstruction of dynamic objects. Our approach improves the mean dynamic mask IoU by 35\% over a state-of-the-art baseline on DAVIS, and reduces the average ATE by 59\% on Bonn and 19\% on TUM relative to the SLAM backbone used in our system. The source code will be made publicly available.

</details>

Abstract: Legged robots equipped with manipulators offer promising capabilities for loco-manipulation tasks in unstructured environments. However, achieving task-consistent end-effector behavior during locomotion remains challenging due to terrain-induced disturbances and the strong coupling between locomotion and manipulation, particularly for small-scale quadruped platforms. A reinforcement-learning based whole-body control framework is proposed for quadruped loco-manipulation on complex terrain. The proposed framework combines a hybrid task representation with task-prioritized whole-body learning to coordinate locomotion and manipulation while preserving task-consistent end-effector behavior. A lightweight quadruped manipulator platform is further developed to validate the proposed framework on a small-scale quadruped system. Real-world experiments demonstrate successful deployment on complex terrains such as slopes up to 30 degrees, stairs, and deformable terrain. These results demonstrate the feasibility of deploying learned whole-body control policy for quadruped loco-manipulation in real-world environments and highlight the importance of task-consistent whole-body coordination for terrain-adaptive loco-manipulation.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning (RL) policies optimized for task rewards may produce behaviors that are efficient but misaligned with human expectations, safety preferences, or intent. This paper presents a reason-guided preference-based reinforcement learning (PbRL) framework that learns a human reward model from trajectory-level feedback. In addition to preference labels, the framework incorporates reason tags explaining why a behavior is preferred, enabling the learned reward to capture both human choices and their underlying rationale. Compared with preference-only reward learning, the reason-guided formulation provides additional supervision for learning human-aligned rewards and improves the interpretability of the resulting policy. The learned human reward is combined with the environment reward during policy optimization, allowing the agent to improve task performance while producing more interpretable, human- aligned, and preference-sensitive behavior

</details>

Abstract: Many manipulation tasks that are effortless for dexterous human hands remain challenging for robotic systems equipped with simple, task-specialized grippers. Industrial platforms commonly rely on end-effectors such as suction cups or parallel-jaw grippers, yet no single mode can reliably satisfy the diverse constraints encountered across different phases of complex manipulation tasks, including pickup, reorientation, insertion, and constrained placement. In this work, we investigate whether complementary simple grippers within a heterogeneous bimanual setup can collectively realize tasks that exceed the capability of any individual gripper alone. Given a single human demonstration specifying the desired object motion, we formulate embodiment-aware execution as a finite-horizon optimization over arm-trajectory assignments. At each segment, the planner determines whether single-arm manipulation or a coordinated dual arm handover is required, jointly reasoning over heterogeneous grasp feasibility, motion constraints, manipulability, and cross-gripper compatibility. Exploiting this formulation, we compute globally consistent assignments with explicit switching regularization. Experiments on a dual Franka FR3 platform equipped with suction and parallel-jaw grippers demonstrate improved feasibility and reliability in tasks that are infeasible under single-gripper execution.

</details>


### [357] [Enhancing Visual Task-Redirection Attacks on Vision-Language-Action Models via Closed-Loop Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23566)
*Chunjin Ye,Guanying Chen,Jianjie Huang,Jianhao Chen,Quanlin Chen,Hanjie Zhao,Haozhe Liang,Tieyun Qian,Xiaochun Cao*

Abstract: Vision-language-action (VLA) models enable language-guided robot manipulation, but remain vulnerable to visual attacks that undermine user control over robot behavior. Prior studies optimize visual perturbations using fixed target supervision. However, the perturbation can change earlier actions and, in turn, the physical states reached later. As the attacked trajectory evolves, this supervision may no longer match the behavior needed at the states actually reached. Accurately matching these targets can therefore fail to advance the attacker-specified task. Even with suitable supervision, reducing average matching error does not ensure sustained task progress. The robot may still fail at a critical stage, and further updates can disrupt previously successful behavior. To address these limitations, we propose TRACO, a framework that enhances existing targeted visual attacks through closed-loop optimization. Its Execution-Driven Supervision rebuilds supervision for the states reached under attack to guide subsequent updates. Within this framework, Progress-Guided Selection focuses updates where progress stalls while maintaining supervision on the earlier behavior needed to reach that point. Experiments across VLA models and simulation environments show improved task-redirection success with TRACO, highlighting the security implications of closed-loop attack optimization.

</details>

Abstract: Measurement-level LiDAR-Inertial-Visual Odometry (LIVO) can achieve high accuracy, and its practical resource requirements depend on the adopted association and map-maintenance strategy. Persistently attaching dense photometric patches or appearance attributes to mapped LiDAR points can introduce additional storage, association, and update costs. This paper presents F-LIVO, a filter-based LIVO framework that couples a visual-inertial subsystem and a LiDAR-inertial subsystem through feature-level point-to-plane constraints between visual points and LiDAR planes. To preserve cross-modal coupling when visual triangulation is weak, we further recover visual feature depth from the LiDAR map through local plane fitting and ray-plane intersection. In the LiDAR update, the covariance of each point-to-plane residual is adaptively scaled according to local planar reliability, thereby reducing overconfident updates on poorly conditioned surfaces. Because the proposed method relies on sparse geometric features rather than persistent dense photometric map attributes, it retains a compact map representation. Experiments on multiple public datasets show competitive, sequence-dependent accuracy. Across the evaluated degradation cases, F-LIVO maintains continuous estimation with comparatively low accumulated end-to-end drift. Under the evaluated resource configuration, it achieves the lowest mean and peak central processing unit (CPU) usage, the lowest peak resident set size (RSS), and the lowest RSS growth rate among the evaluated implementations, while R2LIVE retains a lower mean RSS. The source code will be made publicly available upon acceptance to facilitate reproducible research.

</details>

Abstract: Latent world models learn action-conditioned state transitions in a compact representation space, providing a predictive model for visual planning and control. However, planning with these models typically requires costly test-time optimization over action sequences. We propose to replace this online search with a goal-conditioned inverse model that directly predicts actions from current and goal observations. A key challenge is that action prediction alone neither ensures that goal representations encode action-relevant information nor that predicted actions are consistent with the learned latent prediction model. We introduce Hindsight Inverse JEPA Planning (HIJP), a search-free framework that addresses both aspects during training. HIJP relabels future observations along offline trajectories as goals, providing action supervision across multiple temporal horizons. By retaining gradients through the goal representations, this supervision directly shapes the visual representation. HIJP further feeds predicted actions into the latent predictor model and aligns the resulting next-state representations with encoded successor observations, enforcing action-transition consistency. At inference time, the inverse dynamics model and latent predictor models are applied alternately to generate multi-step action sequences without online search. Across four visual control tasks, HIJP achieves 86.8% overall success and a 1.9-16.5x planning speedup over search-based planning.

</details>

Abstract: Geometric and Semantic Foundation models are becoming increasingly popular tools for generating spatial maps for robots. Recent work (e.g., FOUND-IT) has leveraged these tools to construct real-time, task-driven, and open-set 3D scene graphs from a single uncalibrated camera. However, while classical robot mapping techniques have undergone extensive system demonstrations, limited work has been done to develop both the fundamental and the systems wide advancements needed to integrate these new types of robot spatial memory into a full, useable autonomy pipeline. In this paper, we bridge this gap, and in doing so we also evaluate the effectiveness and limitations of this type of 3D memory. Towards this end, we integrate FOUND-IT into a mobile manipulator platform and demonstrate it is well-suited to support multiple objectives such as object change detection, support for multiple mapping sessions, determination of navigable paths, and directly support object grasping for the task of scene re-arrangement. Furthermore, we provide nuanced insights into the effectiveness and limitations of a monocular, foundation-model-driven 3D scene graph construction system using observations from rigorous on-robot experiments. Code is available at https://github. com/MIT-SPARK/FOUND-IT.

Abstract: Obstacle avoidance for autonomous quadrotors in cluttered environments remains challenging, especially when both dense static obstacles and moving obstacles are present. Existing methods are often tailored to either static or dynamic settings, which always limit their adaptability across diverse environments. This paper presents TransNav, a transformer-based end-to-end navigation method for quadrotor obstacle avoidance in both static and dynamic environments. TransNav employs a patch-based point-cloud encoding scheme that combines local feature extraction with global contextual modeling, enabling compact yet informative environment representation. The method integrates perception and navigation in a single architecture and directly predicts a finite-horizon action sequence, from which the first action is executed. Moreover, we develop an unsupervised learning strategy by incorporating a lightweight point-mass dynamics model, which directly optimizes trajectory-level costs over the predicted sequence through numerical gradient propagation. Thus, the policy can be efficiently trained without expert demonstrations. Simulation and real-world experiments show that the proposed method achieves strong performance in dense static environments and competitive obstacle-avoidance capability in dynamic scenarios, while maintaining low inference cost for onboard deployment.

</details>


### [362] [Learning Robot Metareasoning: Adaptive Computation Budgets for Real-Time Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23662)

Abstract: Planning while moving faces an historical trade-off between deliberation and reactivity. A planner running concurrently with execution needs time to compute, so the robot must commit to an open-loop action chunk for that duration: a longer commitment buys a better plan at the cost of feedback frequency. Existing controllers fix this action commitment, and consequently the computation budget, before deployment. However, the optimal computation time is highly state-dependent (due to factors such as unmodeled dynamics, safety constraints, and marginal optimisation gains), i.e. dynamic, therefore offline static tuning risks leaving systems blind during critical maneuvers or unable to find global effective solutions. We cast the identification of the optimal computation budget as a semi-Markov decision process (SMDP) in which a single decision defines both the robot action commitments and the time allocated to the solver. To solve this, we train a reinforcement-learning policy conditioned on the current state, the executing plan, and inexpensive planner statistics. Across ground-robot navigation, agile quadrotor flight, and contact-rich manipulation, the learned policy changes its budget with the demands of the scenario, discovering efficient reasoning patterns that outperform fixed-budget baselines. https://lorenzomagnino.github.io/learning-robot-metareasoning-review/

</details>


### [363] [Not All Actions Contribute Equally: Learning Precise Progress Estimation from Imprecise Demonstrations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23769)
*Huanqian Wang,Boshi Tang,Botong Xiao,Jie Zhang,Yang Yue,Danhua Tao,Shiji Song,Gao Huang*

Main category: cs.RO

Method: 解析错误

Result: 解析错误

Conclusion: 解析错误

Abstract: Progress estimators play an essential role in providing dense reward signals for reinforcement learning in robotic manipulation, but accurately assessing task progress can be much challenging. Existing methods heavily rely on expert data and naively assume that all actions within a demonstration contribute to task success. This assumption often breaks down in real-world scenarios, especially in high-precision manipulation, where subtle geometric errors can lead to task failure. Therefore, these methods can erroneously interpret human mistakes as meaningful progress, further corrupting progress learning. We argue that a key limitation is the lack of a global prior regarding task evaluation criteria, which is crucial for distinguishing meaningful progress from human mistakes. To this end, we propose PRIME, a novel progress estimator capable of learning precise progress estimation from imprecise demonstrations by aggregating experience across trajectories. PRIME constructs a weighted graph over observed states, connecting states across trajectories based on spatial proximity and visual consistency. Edge weights encode spatial distances as transition costs, and shortest-path search approximates the geodesic distance to success. Changes in this distance naturally distinguish advancement, stagnation, and setbacks without assuming monotonic progress over time. Extensive experiments in both simulation and real world on precise manipulation tasks show that PRIME is consistently effective across diverse tasks, and robust to data with varying degrees of suboptimality. Notably, the advantage of PRIME gets more pronounced in higher-precision scenarios, yielding up to a \(5\times\) improvement in policy success rate over the SFT baselines on charger insertion.

</details>


### [364] [MOSS: Multimodal Origami Swamp Robot System](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23813)
