Abstract: Robotic systems for environmental monitoring and ecological surveying in wetlands and swamps must operate across diverse terrestrial and aquatic conditions. Conventional fixed-geometry wheels cannot adapt their contact geometry to these varying conditions. This work presents MOSS (Multimodal Origami Swamp Robot System), a compact amphibious robotic platform with 3D-printed origami wheels. The compact crank mechanism switches the wheel between contracted and expanded configurations, changing its width, body diameter, and fin orientation simultaneously. The contracted configuration is better suited to efficient rolling on firmer ground, whereas the expanded configuration provides greater contact and terrain engagement on softer surfaces. MOSS was evaluated on soil, sand, mud with varying water content, and water, in addition to in-motion morphing and terrestrial-aquatic transition tests. The more energy-efficient wheel configuration depended on terrain. The contracted configuration achieved a lower cost of transport on firmer soil and mud, whereas the expanded configuration performed better on wetter mud, sand, and water, reducing the cost of transport by up to 29%. Expanded wheels also reduced slip in wet mud and reduced both slip and hub sinkage in sand. MOSS successfully completed configuration changes in $1.95 \pm 0.11$ s and demonstrated continuous locomotion across soil-mud-water transitions. Outdoor trials further demonstrated operation across natural deformable terrain and water. These results show that adaptive wheel geometry provides terrain-dependent locomotion benefits and enables a single compact platform to traverse substantially different terrestrial and aquatic conditions.

</details>

Abstract: Every robot already carries high-resolution contact sensors: its own joints. Legged robots and humanoids exploit them to walk, run, and track whole-body motions without vision, but there the contacts are large and forceful. Is joint sensing also enough for precise, contact-rich assembly, where the robot must find a millimeter-scale opening, mesh gear teeth, or catch a thread through light contact? We study this question by training blind assembly policies that observe only a short history of joint positions, velocities, and their own actions, without visual, force, torque, or tactile sensing. The poses of both the fixture and the part are unknown to the policy, and the grasp is randomized substantially. The policy must therefore assemble the parts through contact, sensed exclusively through its own proprioception. Trained with reinforcement learning entirely in simulation over randomized parts, grasps, and contacts, such policies learn peg insertion, gear meshing, and nut threading. The peg insertion policy transfers zero-shot to the real world and inserts pegs of different shapes and sizes in 73% of trials. Controlled comparisons with matched visual policies in simulation show that blind policies generalize far better to unseen part shapes and sizes, though they can be slower. Training such policies needs thousands of different assemblies, each simulated with its clearance intact, so we also release an open-source generator that builds them procedurally from exact convex pieces for MuJoCo; it is more accurate and faster than convex decomposition or MuJoCo's signed-distance collision. Videos and interactive simulations are at https://younghyopark.me/blind-proprioceptive-assembly/.

</details>

### [366] [DC-TORQ: Fast Assessment of Data Center-Induced Generator Torsional Oscillation Risk Quantification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22572)
*Yingyi Tang,Bin Wang,Junbo Zhao,Qiang Zhang,Pradip Vijayan,Xiaochuan Luo,Tongxin Zheng*

Main category: eess.SY

Motivation: 解析错误

Method: 解析错误

Result: 解析错误

Abstract: The periodic power demand of large data centers can introduce forced oscillations into the power system. When these oscillations contain frequency components near a turbine-generator torsional mode, they may excite sustained shaft torque oscillations with amplitudes exceeding the allowable cyclic torque limits. Characterizing the impact of data center power oscillations on generator shaft torque requires accounting for frequency-dependent network coupling among the data center, the transmission network, and the generators. Although electromagnetic transient (EMT) simulations can capture these interactions in detail, repeated simulations across excitation frequencies and load locations can be computationally demanding. This paper proposes an analytical framework for data center induced torsional oscillations that retains the coupled electrical and shaft dynamics. A detailed generator-network model is established, and closed-form transfer functions that map data center power perturbations to the torque in each shaft section are derived. Admissible shaft torque amplitudes are then translated into frequency-dependent limits on data center power oscillations. The proposed framework enables fast assessment of the shaft torque response while accounting for network dynamics and provides a quantitative screening criterion for candidate data center connection buses. The framework is validated on the IEEE benchmark model and applied to the IEEE 39-bus system.

</details>


### [367] [Modeling and Analysis of Dynamic Coupling Effects in a Free-Floating Space Manipulator](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22558)

Conclusion: 解析错误

Abstract: Motion of a robotic manipulator mounted on a free-floating spacecraft induces base translation and rotation, creating a dynamic coupling that affects end-effector motion. This paper characterizes that coupling in a planar three-link space manipulator. Using momentum conservation, velocity mappings are developed between manipulator joints, the end effector, and the spacecraft base. Singular value decomposition is then used to examine coupling strength, directional anisotropy, and the orientation of the coupled motions. Numerical maps show that coupling strength is invariant to the first joint angle but depends strongly on the relative configuration of the remaining joints. Increasing the spacecraft base mass reduces joint-to-base and end-effector-to-base coupling, while its effect on the joint-to-end-effector mapping is smaller and more dependent on configuration. The translational end-effector-to-base mapping is highly anisotropic in some configurations: its base-velocity ellipse becomes increasingly flattened as the weak coupling mode approaches zero. The principal end-effector and base velocity directions also change their relative alignment across the configuration space, producing predominantly opposing responses in some regions and transverse or approximately codirectional responses in others. Together, these results provide a geometric description of how coupling magnitude and direction vary with manipulator configuration and spacecraft mass.

</details>

Abstract: Friction uncertainty is a significant challenge for autonomous vehicle control, undermining feasibility and performance. This paper proposes a novel two-layer control framework to achieve vehicle motion control under large friction uncertainty by combining optimization-based planning with indirect adaptive robust control (IARC). The upper layer solves a constrained optimal control problem to generate a feasible trajectory that satisfies all the constraints in a time-optimal manner along the desired path. The lower layer, IARC, adapts the unknown parameters related to friction uncertainty and combines model compensation with robust feedback to ensure transient and steady-state tracking performance. An estimation monitor is designed to prevent poor learning when data indicate a model mismatch, while a smooth parameter reset reduces the estimated tire capacity and limits abrupt tightening of constraints. The updated parameters are fed back into the model and constraints in the planner, enabling trajectory replanning as friction conditions change. Simulation results indicate that the proposed method achieves accurate tracking of the desired trajectory and online friction adaptation under large friction uncertainty while ensuring time-optimal vehicle motion. The simulation results demos are available at https://github.com/TedZhou0603/CO-IARC.

</details>

Abstract: This paper proposes a new method to bound the global Lipschitz constant of a neural network (NN). Our formulation uses a two-copy linear fractional transformation (LFT) representation of the NN with stacked signals. We use biased quadratic constraints (QCs) to bound the input/output behavior of the nonlinear activation functions. We then formulate a linear matrix inequality condition, using these biased QCs, to bound the Lipschitz constant of the NN. The best (smallest) Lipschitz bound is computed via a convex, semidefinite program. There are two key distinctions of the proposed two-copy formulation in comparison to existing methods that rely on a single incremental representation with incremental signals. First, the NN bias terms are eliminated in the standard incremental representation, but appear in our formulation. Second, there is a larger class of QCs that can be expressed for the nonlinearities in our formulation than in the incremental representation. We show that our proposed formulation yields less conservative Lipschitz bounds due to these two differences. We illustrate these points via several simple, motivating examples. We also demonstrate the proposed method using a benchmark problem with a NN controller for an inverted pendulum.

</details>

Abstract: This article addresses the control design for systems of several convection-reaction equations distributively coupled to a reaction equation. In typical applications, input and output are associated exclusively with the convection-reaction subsystems. Along the common characteristics of the transport processes, the system can be reduced to a single delayed evolutionary ODE. Based on the latter representation, an inversion-based feedforward control is proposed, whose properties depend on the coupling structure of the original system. While the relative degree of the output is zero in many cases, a relative degree of one is of particular interest for practical applications. To compensate for uncertainties and disturbances, the feedforward control is extended by a tracking controller with a predictor based on the transformed system to account for the resulting delays.

</details>

Abstract: Fast charging of lithium-ion batteries is crucial for electric vehicle adoption, yet it increases safety risks and accelerates battery ageing. To address this, we propose a filter-augmented reinforcement learning (RL) framework for lifelong health-aware fast charging, based on the twin-delayed deep deterministic policy gradient (TD3) algorithm. The RL agent is guided by a reward function that balances charging speed and battery longevity through the incorporation of state of health (SoH) information and an SoH-dependent voltage penalty term derived from a constant--current constant--overpotential charging strategy. Since onboard SoH estimates are inevitably corrupted by noise, two filtering strategies are integrated: an exponential moving average (EMA) filter when noise statistics are unknown, and a Kalman filter when they are available, with the filtered estimate embedded in both the agent's state and reward computation. The RL policy is trained using data from high-fidelity life-cycle simulations. The evaluation uses a published electrochemical and degradation parameterisation for a commercial LG M50 cell. Results show that the proposed method achieves up to 21.4\% more equivalent full cycles (EFCs) than conventional constant current--constant voltage (CC--CV) charging under 2\% SoH estimation noise, and maintains this lifetime advantage even under 5\% noise, while sustaining comparable charging speed.

</details>

Abstract: The rapid growth of artificial intelligence workloads is accelerating the expansion and grid interconnection of data centers (DCs). Existing data center load accommodation (DCLA) assessments primarily focus on grid and resource adequacy and often imply that greater workload flexibility always increases accommodable capacity. However, workload flexibility has a dual effect. Workload deferral and temporal shifting can alleviate network congestion, whereas synchronized checkpointing of training workloads can cause large and rapid load reductions, threatening frequency stability. Therefore, DCLA cannot be determined solely by grid adequacy. This paper develops an optimal DCLA method that jointly accounts for workload quality of service (QoS), checkpoint-induced frequency security (FS), and grid adequacy. First, a QoS-aware model characterizes workload-dependent flexibility while ensuring service feasibility. Second, checkpoint-induced FS constraints are developed for periodic and event-triggered power variations, with key frequency-security limits reformulated in algebraic form. Third, a two-stage lexicographic model is developed to maximize DCLA and minimizes the required energy storage (ES) deployment, and then optimizes system operation under the given DC and ES siting and sizing decisions. Case studies show that the proposed method avoids a 40.3% overestimation of DCLA compared with the grid-adequacy benchmark. QoS is the primary limiting factor, while checkpoint-induced frequency-security constraints become more restrictive as training workloads increase. These findings highlight that reliable DCLA should account for both its grid-support value and the associated QoS and FS risks.

</details>

Abstract: Data centers are emerging as large electricity consumers whose demand can be reshaped through workload scheduling. However, computing flexibility in behind-the-meter facilities does not automatically translate into electrically realizable power flexibility. Ignoring converter and network operating limits may cause converter overloads, protection trips, and interruptions to power supply and computing services. This paper proposes a converter-aware intraday scheduling methodology. First, a task-level mixed-integer linear formulation preserves synchronous training configurations and completion requirements, while Minkowski-sum aggregation and Shapley--Folkman convexification yield a linear inference-pool representation without unit-level binary variables. The latter admits a conditional relative relaxation-gap bound that decreases inversely with pool size. Second, a four-node electrical model links computing demand, on-site injections, and grid exchange to converter and network limits through lifted voltage variables and second-order cone relaxation. Finally, a mixed-integer model coordinates workloads, photovoltaic generation, energy storage, and grid purchases. Case studies show that electrical limits reshape scheduling and renewable utilization, while on-site resources reduce grid dependence. Under the studied conditions, fixed training cannot support full inference service within voltage limits, whereas coordinated scheduling achieves negligible inference shortage and passes verification against the original alternating-current model. These findings demonstrate the role of computing--electrical coordination in realizing the operational value of workload flexibility.

</details>

Abstract: Reachability analysis and set-based estimation for nonlinear systems require set representations capable of capturing nonconvex geometry. Hybrid Zonotopes (HZs) have recently gained attention because they can encode a union of up to $2^n$ polytopic components. Recent work has further shown that several HZ operations can be made \textit{sharp}, meaning that relaxing the binary variables yields exactly the convex hull of the represented set. However, no known HZ construction provides a sharp union of two convex sets that adds only one binary generator and no further continuous generators or equality constraints to those of the operands. This limitation affects scalability in repeated union operations, ReLU neural-network reachability, and nonlinear propagation methods that cover graph sets by unions of simpler sets.
  This paper identifies a connection between the exact closed-form convex-hull operation for Constrained Convex Generators (CCGs) and the binary-generator structure underlying HZs. We propose Hybrid Cones that can encode up to $2^n$ norm-cone components. Hybrid Cones inherit the logical structure of HZs while allowing richer convex components, including polytopic and norm-based sets. They support sharp unions requiring only one added binary variable and can represent unions and intersections of polytopes and ellipsoids. As a key application, we derive an exact representation of the ReLU graph using only two continuous and one binary generators. Numerical experiments for a localization problem with a nonlinear energy measurement function approximated by a neural network and the stability verification of the closed loop of a double integrator corrupted by a nonlinear disturbance with a saturated Linear Quadratic Regulator (LQR) controller show substantial reduction in representation size and reduced conservatism in comparison with HZs.

</details>

Abstract: We address the problem of improving the performance of pre-stabilized networked systems subject to plug-and-play (PnP) operations, namely additions or removals of subsystems. While distributed baseline controllers can preserve stability after a PnP transition, augmenting them with expressive neural policies can invalidate the formal stability guarantees. To overcome this issue, we build on recently proposed Performance Boosting controllers based on the Internal Model Control (IMC) principle, which reduce closed-loop stability to a finite $\ell_2$-gain requirement on an auxiliary operator. In the distributed setting, this operator is formed by interconnecting local neural operators that exchange information only between neighboring subsystems. We then derive free parametrizations of this interconnection, namely explicit maps from unconstrained trainable variables to interconnected neural operators satisfying a prescribed network gain bound for every parameter value. The obtained distributed neural controller can therefore be trained by unconstrained gradient-based optimization without stability constraints or projection steps, while retaining the prescribed gain bound at every training iterate. We develop two complementary parametrizations. A neighbor-based compositional parametrization uses local coupling bounds, so that after a PnP event only subsystems whose bounds change need to update their gain parameters. A network-norm parametrization instead uses a bound on the overall coupling matrix and can reduce conservatism, at the price of requiring network-wide information. A PnP DC-microgrid case study shows that the proposed neural layer improves voltage transients under topology changes and load variations while preserving nominal closed-loop stability.

</details>

Abstract: Proactive defense offers a means to enhance power-system cybersecurity through controlled modifications of system configurations. One example is moving target defense (MTD), which uses distributed flexible AC transmission system (D-FACTS) devices to perturb transmission-line reactances against stealthy false data injection attacks. Existing MTD methods primarily rely on deterministic optimization, producing a single perturbation plan for each operating condition. However, repeated or highly correlated plans may expose predictable system configurations that attackers can learn and exploit. To address this limitation, this paper reformulates MTD as a conditional generative problem that produces diverse, secure, and physically feasible perturbation candidates. Learning such a distribution is challenging because MTD decisions jointly involve selecting branches, determining perturbation magnitudes, and coordinating adjustments across the network. To make this problem tractable, we factorize the decision distribution into three sequential conditional stages: branch selection, magnitude allocation, and continuous refinement. Graph-conditioned diffusion models are employed to capture the discrete and continuous decisions while accounting for network dependencies. Furthermore, a - objective is incorporated into diffusion training to favor candidates with desirable defense effectiveness and operational costs. Numerical studies on the IEEE 118- and 300-bus systems show that the proposed framework achieves AC feasibility comparable to that of optimization-based MTD baselines and higher - values, while reducing online computation time by an order of magnitude. The generated candidate pool remains effective under changing operating conditions and device availability, providing diverse high-value alternatives for flexible online cyber defense.

</details>

Abstract: This paper provides an extensive dataset for commercial 58 Ah prismatic NMC cells. The dataset comprises three types of data: characterization, ageing, and cell teardown. Characterization data include tests such as GITT, HPPC, and EIS, among others. Ageing data were obtained from 41 cells subjected to 38 distinct ageing conditions, including application-surrogate drive cycles. Recurring reference performance tests and EIS measurements track the evolution of each cell. Teardown data were obtained from pristine and aged (post-mortem) cells. They include material analyses and half-cell measurements. This single dataset supports analytics, model development (both machine-learning and physics-based), benchmarking, and techno-economic analysis. Several example analyses are carried out to show the usability of the data. For instance, the paper shows how lithium stripping can be detected from the voltage response of a short discharge applied after each charge. The data and the processing and analysis scripts are openly provided alongside this paper.

Abstract: This paper introduces Quantum-Inspired Energy-Neutral (QIEN) UAV Trajectory for WPT-Enabled Industrial HRLLC, a novel framework for mobility-integrated trajectory and resource optimization in solar-powered uncrewed aerial vehicle (UAV) relay systems supporting autonomous mining robots in post-disaster environments. We formulate a mixed-integer nonlinear program (MINLP) that jointly optimizes UAV trajectory, finite blocklength allocation, transmit power, and wireless power transfer (WPT) over discrete time horizons to maximize aggregate data throughput. The system operates under strict extreme hyper-reliable low-latency communication (HRLLC) constraints, energy sustainability requirements via solar harvesting, and continuous robot power delivery. To solve this large-scale optimization problem, QIEN employs a hybrid Cooperative Quantum-behaved Particle Swarm Optimization (Co-QPSO) algorithm using quantum tunneling mechanisms and a heterogeneous swarm architecture. Our simulation results demonstrate that the proposed QIEN framework significantly outperforms benchmark algorithms, achieving successful convergence while maintaining energy-neutral operation and satisfying all reliability, power, and mobility constraints.

</details>

Abstract: While the widespread deployment of Large Language Models (LLMs) has significantly driven up power demands in computing clusters, the unique execution characteristics of LLM inference offer a novel opportunity to utilize these clusters as controllable loads. In this paper, we investigate the potential of LLM inference cluster to provide frequency regulation services through inference request scheduling. First, we model the problem as a hierarchical Markov decision process that includes request-to-replica dispatching and token-level resource allocation. Then, we introduce a reward redistribution network to address the temporal asynchrony between millisecond-level scheduling decisions and second-level frequency regulation signals. Furthermore, we develop an optimization framework based on a mean-field approximation to enable coordination among a large number of replicas under high request concurrency. Simulations using real-world data demonstrate that the proposed approach accurately tracks frequency regulation signals without compromising service-level objectives.

</details>

Abstract: In this paper, we propose a novel joint optimization framework for autonomous, quantum-enabled UAV repeater networks designed to support hyper-reliable low-latency communications (HRLLC) in challenging environments such as post-explosion chemical plant operations. The proposed system uses a symmetric dual-UAV architecture with three sequential quantum channels operating under strict energy harvesting (EH) constraints. We formulate a highly challenging non-convex mixed-integer non-linear programming (MINLP) problem. This model jointly determines qubit allocation, 2-D uncrewed aerial vehicle (UAV) spatial positioning (distance and altitude), and transmit powers to minimize the total decoding error. Crucially, our framework explicitly models real-world quantum physics, including decoherence, gate imperfections, and entanglement degradation. Since the objective function is demonstrably non-convex, to efficiently solve the MINLP, we propose a quantum-resource-optimized GA (QRO-GA). The results of our numerical simulations show that the QRO-GA yields better results than benchmark methods and it is highly efficient, with the convergence speed significantly increasing with the total qubit allocation. Convergence is achieved in as few as 5 iterations for higher qubit allocation. Moreover, the results reveal a critical U-shaped tradeoff, proving that an optimal latency distribution within a strict $80\ μ$s transmission window is strictly required to maintain HRLLC reliability. In addition, we find that the QRO-GA successfully optimizes the 2-D UAV positioning to minimize total decoding error. Finally, it balances consumed and harvested power to achieve net-zero power UAVs system.

</details>

Abstract: This paper presents a novel multiclass first order macroscopic traffic model for mixed-autonomy in signalized urban networks, called MIXSim. Human driven vehicles (HDVs) and Connected and Automated Vehicles (CAVs) are represented as separate classes while origin-destination information is retained through commodity specific states. The local vehicle class composition and class specific driving behaviour characteristics determine the parameters of a composition-dependent triangular fundamental diagram. At signalized intersections, the composition of movement demand additionally determines the saturation flow rate, while the realized movement flow is constrained by demand, effective green time, and downstream receiving capacity. Numerical experiments investigate different CAV penetration levels and CAV time gaps, together with alternative signal control strategies. The numerical experiments illustrate how changes in CAV penetration and CAV time gap affect network performance under the tested conditions, and demonstrate the use of MIXSim for comparing signal control strategies across different mixed traffic regimes.

</details>

Abstract: Robust control uses the error between a known model and the true system as a robustness margin. However, this error is usually unknown: conservative estimates restrict feasibility and performance, while underestimates can result in violations of the control property. We estimate this robustness margin from online data using e-values, a statistical tool for hypothesis testing, in the setting of discrete-time control barrier functions (CBFs). Here, we measure the robustness margin as the difference between the observed CBF and the model-based CBF prediction. We then design a robust CBF controller in three steps by: (1) selecting a set of candidate margins, (2) obtaining validity certificates for these candidates via sequential tests based on e-values, and (3) updating the CBF controller with the smallest certified candidate. The deployed tests accumulate evidence against the hypothesis that an exceedance risk, the probability of the robustness margin exceeding the candidate margin, remains at or above a pre-specified failure probability $α$. Our tests are anytime valid and thus enable the use of certified candidates in the CBF controller, guaranteeing that the probability that the exceedance risk of a certified margin exceeds $α$ is at most a user-chosen confidence $δ$. We validate our approach on navigation problems across different environments with visualizations in NVIDIA Isaac Sim.

</details>

Abstract: Lyapunov exponents (LEs) are fundamental metrics from nonlinear dynamical systems theory that quantify the average exponential rate of divergence or convergence of nearby trajectories in phase space. Over the past three decades, LEs have emerged as powerful tools across a wide range of power system problems, including transient and voltage stability assessment, chaos detection and mitigation, short-term load and renewable energy forecasting, and fault diagnosis. This paper presents a comprehensive review of these applications, covering more than 165 publications spanning from the early demonstrations of chaos in simplified machine models (1990s) to the most recent data-driven and machine-learning-augmented approaches applied to inverter-based resource (IBR)-dominated grids (2024--2026). The review is organized into four principal application domains: (i) stability analysis and real-time monitoring, (ii) chaos characterization and control, (iii) forecasting and predictability analysis, and (iv) protection, fault diagnosis, and condition monitoring. For each domain, we trace the evolution of methods, compare model-based and data-driven LE estimation strategies, identify key benchmarks and validation gaps, and outline the most pressing open research problems.

</details>

Abstract: A gradient-based solver that enforces a Signal Temporal Logic (STL) specification uses a smooth approximation of its quantitative semantics, the robustness. In safety-critical trajectory optimization, this approximation must have two properties. First, it must be sound, that is, nonnegative only on trajectories that satisfy the specification. Second, at each temporal operator, the weight of its gradient on the violating samples must stay bounded away from zero, however long the horizon. The approximations in use, including the log-sum-exp, its sound variant, and the generalized-mean robustness, do not have both. We introduce lower approximations of the minimum and the maximum whose gradients are sparsemax projections, and we prove that the robustness built from them has both. It lies below the exact robustness and is therefore sound, and its error has a bound that is independent of the number of samples and known before optimization. The gradient of each approximation is exactly zero on every sample beyond a data-dependent threshold, so the weight on violating samples a gap beyond the others has a positive lower bound at every horizon. On a planar unicycle and on a seven-joint manipulator beside a person in simulation, the log-sum-exp approximation is positive on a violating trajectory. As the horizon of the manipulator's task grows from 10 to 80 s, the gradient weight of the sound variant on the violating samples falls from 0.22 to 0.04, while that of sparsemax stays at 1. In a constrained trajectory optimization of the unicycle, the constraint on the log-sum-exp robustness returns a violating trajectory, whereas the constraint on the sparsemax robustness returns a trajectory that satisfies the specification with the required margin.

</details>

Abstract: Accurate prognosis prediction for head and neck cancer typically necessitates integrating highly heterogeneous clinical and pathological evidence by sophisticated multimodal fusion. However, existing fusion techniques are usually grounded in case-generic clinical rationales about complementary synergies across modalities, underestimating case-specific variance. In this study, we propose a prognosis prediction framework that explicitly incorporates Case-specific Priors to guide Multimodal fusion (named CPM). It leverages broad medical knowledge of LLMs to characterize each case's (case-specific) intra-modal contexts and inter-modal relations, as well as case-generic multimodal rationale, as priors, enabling three-stage priors-guided multimodal fusion: (i) Intra-modal Context-guided Calibration (IntraCC) that leverages case-specific intra-modal priors to guide feature calibration within each modality; (ii) Inter-modal Relation-guided Interaction (InterRI) that leverages case-specific inter-modal priors to guide pair-wise feature interaction in modality pairs; and (iii) Multimodal Rationale-guided Aggregation (MultiRA) that leverages case-generic multimodal priors to guide final feature aggregation over all modalities. On the well-established HANCOCK challenge dataset involving four distinct medical modalities, our CPM achieved a mean AUC of 83.98% for predicting 5-year overall survival and 2-year recurrence-free survival, improving upon the baseline by 7.58%, pioneeringly demonstrating the effectiveness of LLM-derived medical priors in guiding multimodal fusion for prognosis prediction. The code is available at https://github.com/yuelin421/CPM.

</details>

Abstract: Promptable segmentation models turn one 3D bounding box into an instance mask. Box placement therefore limits how fast microscopy volumes with thousands of objects are annotated. Each box must be checked in several synchronized viewers, but napari-bbox shares no state between viewers. An edit must update every viewer and slows down with more boxes. napari-TurboBox instead holds one shared copy of the boxes for all viewers. In a four-viewer layout, napari-TurboBox needs a few milliseconds per mouse move to update the three slice viewers up to $10^4$ boxes. The same update in napari-bbox leaves the 33 ms budget beyond about $10^3$ boxes and takes 159 ms at $10^4$. Boxes stay axis-aligned and export to COCO and YOLO as per-slice 2D boxes.
  napari-TurboBox is a pure-Python plugin for napari 0.6.6 to 0.9. The code is available at https://github.com/N-Friederich/napari-turbobox under the Apache 2.0 licence, and version 0.1.0 is archived at https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.23090828 on Zenodo.

Abstract: Accurate prognosis in non-small cell lung cancer (NSCLC) benefits from integrating computed tomography (CT) and histopathological whole-slide images (WSIs), which capture complementary macroscopic and microscopic disease characteristics. Fusing these modalities is difficult: their features come from different pretrained foundation-model encoders and lack spatial correspondence, and both are sensitive to institution-specific acquisition and staining, causing multimodal models to overfit site-specific artifacts and generalize poorly. We present CRUX (CRoss-modal Unification via teXt anchoring), a fusion framework that uses clinical text---encoded by a biomedical language model---as an institution-invariant semantic anchor. Because clinical variables follow standardized classification systems, their embeddings occupy a representation space that is consistent across institutions, providing a stable reference frame for aligning heterogeneous imaging features. CRUX introduces a text-mediated cross-modal bridge in which CT- and pathology-derived text-query summaries exchange information within a compact text semantic space through a learnable hub token, avoiding direct image-space cross-attention that would propagate domain-specific artifacts. We evaluate on 2,157 NSCLC patients from five hospitals, holding out one hospital (N=446) entirely for external validation. CRUX attains an external concordance index (C-index) of 0.761, the highest among all unimodal, bimodal, and trimodal configurations. Notably, direct CT-WSI fusion degrades under distribution shift, whereas text anchoring restores and improves cross-institutional generalization. These results indicate that language-model-encoded clinical text is an effective structured prior for robust, generalizable multimodal cancer prognosis.

</details>

Abstract: End-to-end learned image coding (LIC) has achieved substantial improvements in rate--distortion (R--D) performance, yet practical deployment requires addressing a broader set of system-level constraints. This article provides a deployment-oriented review of LIC, with particular attention to the transition from high-performance neural compression models to practical codecs. We first summarize the basic end-to-end coding framework, including learned transforms, quantization, entropy modeling, and R--D optimization. We then review practical extensions that are increasingly important for deployment, including variable-rate coding and rate control, integer-precision inference and cross-platform consistency, robustness and communication resilience, lightweight and hardware-aware implementation, and standardization and interoperability. To study these issues under a common setting, we further introduce TinyLIC as an open benchmark and reproducible case study for rate--distortion--complexity analysis. Experimental results illustrate the trade-offs among compression efficiency, computational complexity, numerical consistency, and robustness. In particular, TinyLIC with approximately 10 KMACs/pixel decoding complexity substantially outperforms the H.265/HEVC image-profile anchor on Kodak, while the 50 KMACs/pixel configuration achieves performance comparable to the H.266/VVC image-profile anchor on several test sets. The review highlights practical constraints and design trade-offs that remain central to the deployment and interoperability of learned image codecs. Materials are accessible at https://njuvision.github.io/TinyLIC.

</details>

Abstract: Small-area forest plantations play a vital role in the socioeconomic well-being of farmers in Southeast Asia. Most plantations are established on former agricultural land, often on land less suitable for agriculture. Plantations that are converted from natural forest have adverse impacts on biodiversity. Mapping small-area plantations is thus important to understand the dynamics of forest cover in Southeast Asia and to study the social, economic, and ecological effects of this important land cover and land use change. While the small size of forest plantations makes it difficult to detect them using moderate resolution satellite sensors, the problem is exacerbated by the high degree of mixing between plantations, surrounding vegetation, and other land covers, which often show variegated responses in satellite signals across space and time. In this paper, we study the problem of mapping small-area forest plantations in East and West Godavari districts of Andhra Pradesh, India using deep learning methods. Remotely sensed cloud-free data from the Harmonized Landsat Sentinel-2 S10 product were classified using a pixel-level neural network and training data labeled using a field-based survey in concert with expert aerial photo interpretation. We compare the performance of deep learning methods with a baseline random forest classifier in our study region of 21543 sq. km over a period of 3 years and analyze the differences in the results across land cover classes and seasons.

</details>

Abstract: Deformable image registration is a fundamental challenge in medical image analysis. Recent deep learning-based methods typically adopt either one-pass architectures that complete registration in a single network iteration, or recursive architectures that refine registration through multiple passes. While recursive methods often improve accuracy, they incur substantial computational overhead and slower inference speed. In this paper, we challenge the necessity of recursion for high-performance deformable registration and propose M2CG, a one-pass registration network with Multi-level Multi-space Contextual Guidance. It performs multi-step progressive pyramid registration within a single iteration, guided at each step by contextual cues from three levels (previous, current, and subsequent steps) and four spaces (image space, image feature space, segmentation space, and segmentation feature space). Our M2CG supports both unsupervised (without any labels) and semi-supervised (with segmentation labels for partial training data) registration, and has achieved state-of-the-art performance across two well-benchmarked inter-/intra-patient medical registration tasks with eight public datasets. Our findings suggest that the current reliance on recursion may stem from the absence of sufficient contextual cues that comprehensively characterize the remaining distance to the target alignment.

</details>

Abstract: We derive the optically aligned system of image-scaling operators $\mathbf{K}_s$ that reproduces optical zoom (exact zoom-out, canonical zoom-in), by identifying a Huygens-like consistency principle in which each pixel acts as a secondary source. The system is determined in closed form by the camera's point spread function (PSF), itself the operator $\mathbf{K}_0$. The operators compose, $\mathbf{K}_{s'} \circ \mathbf{K}_s = \mathbf{K}_{ss'}$ (with band projection for down-up), and account for both upscaling and downscaling, which form a Moore-Penrose pseudoinverse pair, $\mathbf{K}_{1/s} = \mathbf{K}_s^\dagger$. We show that image scaling, as routinely practiced, is neither optically faithful nor internally consistent; for example, common anti-aliasing rules generally correspond to no camera, and two resizes by a factor of $1/2$ generally do not equal one resize by a factor of $1/4$. Gaussian scale-space kernels cannot be exact finite-aperture PSFs. Gaussian scale-space is an idealized special case of what emerges here: a generalized scale-space for sampled images, derived entirely from camera optics, bidirectional, and not limited to Gaussian kernels. The system goes beyond scaling and supports composable and invertible subpixel geometric transformations. We empirically validate its optical predictions with real captures and noisy optics simulations, and demonstrate its practical impact on the stability of tasks such as super-resolution and classification.

</details>

Abstract: With the widespread application of infrared object detection technologies in military domains, intelligent infrared adversarial camouflage capabilities have become critical. Existing physical attack methods predominantly employ static thermal suppression strategies, suffering from limited environmental adaptability, insufficient attack robustness, and inadequate visual concealment. This study proposes Adv-Camou, an emissivity-based physical dynamic adversarial patch that achieves paradigm-shifting advancements from static to dynamic attacks through algorithm-material co-innovation. First, a multi-level loss function based on feature confusion mechanisms is designed to deceive detectors by attenuating target saliency and enforcing background feature alignment. Second, adaptive physical transformations and differentiable binarization optimization are integrated to address physical robustness and realizability constraints. A 6*4 array of reversible electrochromic emissivity device is developed and tested across a range of background scenarios, enabling scene-adaptive dynamic camouflage through independent unit control. Experiments demonstrate that this method achieves 95.59% attack success rate (ASR), reducing average precision (AP) by 60.51%. Physical validations show that static adversarial patches attain 76.55% average ASR under multi-view, multi-range, and multi-spectral conditions, while dynamic patches enhance ASR up to 91.55% in complex environments (wilderness/urban) through adaptive switching. The seamless digital-to-physical transfer via tunable emissivity devices transcends the limitations of traditional static camouflage by establishing a dynamic infrared regulation capability. This advancement enhances the battlefield survivability of military assets and demonstrates transformative potential for adaptive camouflage in practical combat scenarios.

</details>

Abstract: Serial MRI is routinely performed in current clinical practice for disease surveillance and applications such as MR-guided radiotherapy. However, long MRI scan times increase costs and limit patient access, making accelerated image reconstruction of broad clinical importance. Previous scans of the same patient contain image information highly correlated with the current exam, yet existing reconstruction methods rarely exploit this information. Major challenges in the effective use of prior scans include inter-visit misregistration, pathological changes, and image phase variation. In this work, we propose Longitudinal Guided Group Sparsity (LGGS), an interpretable deep-learning framework for longitudinally informed MRI reconstruction derived from classical group sparsity. The prior scan is incorporated into the reconstruction regularizer through learned phase-invariant circulant attention that identifies corresponding features across exams. This formulation provides robustness to local in-plane and through-plane misregistration as well as inter-visit phase variation. Across acceleration rates, LGGS achieves state-of-the-art performance on longitudinal T1-weighted multi-coil brain MRI data, outperforming the evaluated unguided and longitudinally informed reconstruction methods, with substantially fewer learned parameters. We further evaluated the contributions of multiple guide slices and phase-invariant similarity and demonstrated that accelerated prior images can also serve as effective guides with minimal degradation in reconstruction quality.

</details>

Abstract: Single-particle cryogenic electron microscopy (cryo-EM) is an imaging technique capable of reconstructing high-resolution three-dimensional (3D) structure of biological macromolecules from a set of noisy projection images taken at random viewing directions. The mathematical model for cryo-EM images typically assumes that each image contains exactly one particle, which is centered at the origin. However, many experimental datasets contain particle images which include multiple particles: one central particle and a few crowding particles. This deviation from the model leads to inaccurate estimation of image statistics, such as the low-order image moments. To account for this, we propose a method to estimate image moments while correcting for the effect of crowding particles. This method can be run directly from a set of cryo-EM micrographs with picked particles, and we demonstrate its efficacy on a wide array of synthetic data.

</details>

Abstract: Practical non-line-of-sight (NLOS) measurements are affected by noise and deviations from idealized light transport, which can degrade reconstruction with conventional light-cone transform (LCT) methods. We propose the Measurement-Adaptive Light-Cone Transform (MALCT), a physics-guided framework that retains the efficient Fourier-domain inversion structure of LCT. {Within a unified variational formulation, MALCT combines the analytical response with a shared learned response through measurement-conditioned weighting and predicts a measurement-conditioned, frequency-dependent regularization map.} An attention-enhanced spatio-temporal feature extraction module captures long-range temporal dependencies and local spatial correlations to support reconstruction under low-signal conditions. Experiments on synthetic data and real measurements from different NLOS systems demonstrate improved reconstruction quality. Controlled evaluations further assess robustness to temporal-response broadening and reduced acquisition time. MALCT also preserves scene structures with spatial sampling grids down to $32\times32$, supporting its potential for NLOS imaging under limited acquisition budgets.

</details>

Abstract: Low-light image enhancement (LLIE) requires more than brightness amplification because real low-light images often exhibit spatially varying illumination, amplified noise, color distortion, and local detail loss. Recent SSM- and Mamba-based restorers provide efficient long-range modeling, yet the route for introducing illumination priors into such backbones remains insufficiently studied. This study addresses safe illumination conditioning for SSM-based LLIE through a bottleneck-side PromptAdapter. A lightweight illumination estimator first predicts a single-channel illumination map, which is then converted into prompt tokens and injected through a residual sidecar adapter at the bottleneck, thereby avoiding direct modification of the state-space core. Under a locked three-seed evaluation protocol on LOL-v1, LOLv2-Real, and LOLv2-Synthetic, the PromptAdapter improves over the Base model by 0.94 dB, 1.24 dB, and 0.93 dB in PSNR, respectively, while also improving SSIM, LPIPS, and masked dark-region metrics. The clearest real-data evidence appears on LOLv2-Real. A Learned-Fixed control on LOL-v1 indicates that the present results support the utility of the sidecar conditioning pathway more strongly than a strict claim of illumination-conditioned superiority, and the LOLv2-Synthetic result remains stability-conditioned. These findings suggest that, in SSM-based LLIE, conditioning design can be as important as the conditioning signal itself.

</details>

Abstract: Clutter suppression is a critical preprocessing step in ultrasound blood flow imaging, yet conventional singular value decomposition (SVD) filtering depends on data-specific eigenvalue cutoffs that can remove slow flow along with tissue clutter. Here, we introduce adaptive eigenspectrum remapping (AER), a general framework in which clutter is suppressed by continuously remapping, rather than truncating, the eigenvalue spectrum of the spatiotemporal covariance, and we investigate zero-phase component analysis (ZCA) matrix whitening as an instance of AER for transcranial ultrasound localization microscopy (ULM). Rather than discarding eigenspaces, ZCA-based AER whitens the eigenvalue spectrum, reducing the dominance of coherent clutter, including skull reverberation, while retaining weak flow signals. We evaluate AER on both temporal and spatial covariance matrices in mouse brain datasets acquired with long temporal ensembles. Compared with SVD filtering, ZCA improves microbubble contrast and vascular image quality, increases trajectory density across low- and high-concentration regimes, and reveals more low-velocity trajectories in ULM velocity maps. Spatial AER further maintains reconstruction quality across extended ensembles and enables fuller single capillary reporter (SCaRe) reconstructions, including connected artery-to-capillary-to-vein tracks. These results support AER, here instantiated as ZCA whitening, as a data-adaptive alternative to manually thresholded SVD filtering for high-resolution microvascular imaging.

</details>

Abstract: This study investigates CT-to-PET image synthesis using TransNSNet, a Transformer-enhanced multi-scale adversarial framework. A paired CT-PET dataset was curated from the public non-small cell lung cancer (NSCLC) Radiogenomics collection through patient screening, slice-level matching, preprocessing, and patient-level train-test separation. The proposed framework generates PET-like predictions at multiple spatial scales and applies coarse-to-fine reconstruction supervision, while TransPatchGAN introduces contextual feature interaction into adversarial evaluation. A progressive ablation study compares Pix2Pix, NSNet-Base, NSNet-D, and TransNSNet. Image-level performance was summarized over a fixed convergence window (epochs 80-101) from one training run per model. TransNSNet achieved the best mean value in nine of ten metrics, whereas NSNet-Base achieved the highest visual saliency-induced index. For fixed-fraction high-intensity regions, NSNet-D was marginally higher in slice-level recall and balanced accuracy, but the equal-weight patient-level values were nearly identical and the highest Dice values shifted to TransNSNet. These differences were small relative to between-patient variability. In the separate adaptive patient-level analysis (n=10), no pairwise model difference remained statistically significant after Holm correction. TransNSNet also had a substantially smaller parameter footprint than the original next-scale models. These findings indicate promising image-quality trends, but they do not establish statistical superiority or clinical validity; synthesized PET-like images should not be interpreted as substitutes for clinically acquired PET.

</details>
